- **CSS columns or fragmentation** (a multi-column container sized to a page). Chromium has no reliable page-box fragmentation for editable content, widows and orphans cannot be tuned, mirrored margins cannot differ per column, and the running head and number would still need per-page elements.
- **Padding each block by hand to fill the page** (no spacers): moves the text of a page into the foot margin when an edit changes a block above it before the next measurement.

**Known limits:** breaks are estimates unless Typst's marks fit the text (see "Exact page breaks in Page view"; the rest of this list is about the estimate) (the print font is not in the webview unless it is installed, footnotes and images are approximate; the drop cap is not re-indented after its first line as in the PDF), the hyphen at a page break is a zero-width widget at the spacer drawn only when the browser hyphenates (`hyphens: auto`) mid-word, and a block that cannot split (a table, an image) taller than a page overflows its card.

## Measured page numbers from Typst (2026-10-28)

**Decision:** while Page view is on, the app asks Typst where each chapter starts and how long the book is, without producing a PDF, and uses that for the status bar's book total ("37 pages" without the "≈") and for the number printed on the open chapter's first page. Everything else about Page view (the breaks inside a chapter, the cards) is still the in-editor estimate; only the numbers that depend on the whole book come from Typst.

**How it works:**

- `packages/export-pdf` marks every page entry in the Typst source (`metadata((id, kind))` on `<chapter-start>`, one invisible `<numbering-arabic>` mark where numbering restarts) and ends the document with `<page-report>`: `(total, starts: ((id, kind, page, number), ...))`. `measurePages` builds the same source as `renderPdf` (shared `prepareDocument`), runs `typst query ... --field value --one`, and validates the JSON (`parsePageReport`). A query compiles the layout only, so no PDF is encoded.
- `TypstRunner.query` is implemented by the Node runner and by the `typst_query` Tauri command (same binary frame as `typst_compile` with `selector` in place of `pdfA`; same temp dir, `--root`, font paths, 120 s timeout and error kinds). It returns Typst's stdout bytes.
- The `measurePages` store action (`state/project/output/exports.ts`) reads the pages like an export, but takes the open page's draft instead of its saved file, never takes the export lock, and keeps the last report keyed by a hash of template, trim size, fonts and the book's content, so an unchanged book costs no Typst run.
- `useMeasuredPages` (mounted by the status bar's `PageEstimate`) waits 2 s after the book last changed and only while Page view is on, then stores the report in `state/pages/pageMeasure.ts`. Any change marks the report stale at once; the status bar then falls back to the estimate. `PageFrame` takes the chapter's first page number from the last report even when it is stale, so page cards do not reflow on every keystroke.
- A failure (Typst not installed, a compile error) is kept in the store and shown nowhere: the word-count estimate stays, as before.

**Rejected:**

- **Reading the page count from a rendered PDF** (pdf.js, or the Node-only `pdfinfo`): needs a new dependency or poppler, and gives a total but not where each chapter starts.
- **A second `typst compile` that writes a report file:** a PDF encode per measure for data Typst can return directly from `query`.
- **Embedding the Typst crate:** see "M2 print side"; the same reasons apply.
- **Measuring on every keystroke or on a timer regardless of Page view:** a measure is seconds of CPU for a book. It runs only while the author is looking at pages and only once the text has been still.
- **Reusing the export lock:** a background measure must not make Export fail with "already running".

**Limits:** the total counts from the first page numbered 1 to the last page, back matter included (the estimate counted chapters only); a measure of a very large book takes seconds, and the numbers lag the text by that long.

## Typst-rendered print preview (2026-10-29)

**Decision:** the print preview shows the real PDF. `renderPreview` (`state/project/output/preview.ts`) runs the same Typst job as the export (`renderPdf`, same fonts, cover, bleed and crop marks) on the book as it is now (`state/project/output/typstBook.ts`: the open page's draft stands in for its saved file, shared with `measurePages`), keeps the bytes in memory only (an LRU of 3, keyed by a hash of template, trim size, fonts, bleed, marks, cover and content) and never writes to the book or takes the export lock. `usePrintPdf` asks after 250 ms of unchanged choices. `TypstSpread` opens the bytes with pdf.js (already a dependency for the reference viewer; loaded when the first preview opens, handed a copy of the buffer because pdf.js may take it over) and draws facing pages (page 1 alone on the right, then (2, 3), (4, 5)...) with Previous/Next; margins are outlined from `pageBox` (`packages/export-pdf/src/typst/bleed.ts`, the same numbers as the Typst page setup), not on the cover and its blank back. While Typst works the old pages stay, dimmed, with a progress line.

**Fallback:** when Typst is missing or the book does not compile, `ApproxSpread` (the former estimate: template margins, face, word-count page count) is shown with a notice that gives the reason, so the preview never goes blank.

**Exact length of a chapter.** The page report (`<page-report>`) now also carries `end`, the last page of each entry (its paired `<chapter-end>` mark). A chapter's page count is `end - page + 1` (`reportChapterPages`), so a blank verso before the chapter is not counted. While Page view is on and the report is current, the status bar's chapter total is Typst's (" · Page ≈ 3 of 12": the cursor's page is the editor's layout, so it keeps the "≈" unless the breaks are exact too, see "Exact page breaks in Page view"), and the book total was already Typst's.

**Rejected:**

- **Rasterising on the Rust side** (a PDF library crate): a new dependency where pdf.js is already shipped.
- **Writing the preview PDF into the book folder** (stale files, and "never overwrite user files" needs a backup story for something that is not the author's).
- **Typesetting on every edit**: a run is seconds; the preview is a screen the author opens, and results are cached by content.
- **Exact page breaks inside the editor from this slice.** That needs a Typst mark at each block and where a paragraph splits, mapped back to ProseMirror positions, with `paginate` taking forced breaks; breaks at block level only would be wrong wherever a paragraph straddles a page. Done the next day, see "Exact page breaks in Page view" below.

## Exact page breaks in Page view (2026-10-29)

**Decision:** while Page view is on, the editor's page breaks inside a chapter are Typst's, to the word, wherever the measure's marks fit the text; the cursor's page in the status bar is then exact (" · Page 3 of 12", no "≈"). Where they do not fit, or before the first report, the estimate is used exactly as before (the chip says "approximate" or "breaks as in the PDF").

**The marks** (`packages/export-pdf`, `PdfOptions.blockMarks`, set only by `measurePages` for the store's measure and the preview's layout query; renders and exports never carry them). `buildTypstDocument` writes `#bm-s(chapter, block, kind)` at the start of every top-level block of every chapter (not front or back matter) and `#wm(w)` before each word of an ordinary paragraph after the first; `<page-report>` lists them from `query(<bm>)`, compressed in Typst (every `s` mark, but a word mark only when its page differs from the mark before it, so a 100,000-word chapter reports a few hundred tuples). `parsePageReport` turns that into `PageReport.blocks[entryId]` = `{ block, kind, page, splits: [{ word, page }] }`. The block `kind` (`p` ordinary paragraph, `q` any other paragraph and the drop cap paragraph, `h` heading, `s` scene break, `o` anything else) lets the editor check that the text it maps back to is the text that was typeset. Words are counted by `wordStarts` in `packages/shared` (a word starts at the first character after a space, a tab, a hard break or the block start, offsets are ProseMirror's flat ones) which the exporter and the editor both call.

What Typst 0.15 does with a mark, measured with the real binary and checked by `marks.render.test.ts` (it compares the text of every page with and without marks for three templates and a drop cap chapter, and every reported split against `pdftotext`): marks change nothing on the page (the layout, page count and every page's text are identical); a mark in the middle of a paragraph that sits where a line breaks stays on the line before the break; a mark after the last line of a block at the foot of a page is moved to the next page. So: the first word mark seen on a new page is the one before the page's second word, and the page begins one word earlier (`parseMarks`); there are no end marks (they would say "next page" for a block that ends exactly at the foot of one), and a block's last page is the last split's page. Typst's widow and orphan control keeps at least two lines of a paragraph on each page, which is what makes the second word's mark always exist. A measure with marks costs about 1.1 s more on a 100,000-word chapter (231 pages, 0.6 s without).

**What the editor does** (`lib/page/exactBreaks.ts`, `editor/pagination/exact.ts`, `plugin.ts`). `exactBreaks` turns the report's blocks into page breaks in document terms (before block n, or before word w of paragraph n), or null when they cannot be trusted: the report's blocks must be the document's blocks of the same kind (blank paragraphs the exporter skips may be anywhere, nothing else may), pages must follow each other with no gap, a split must name a word the paragraph has, and a block whose end the report cannot see (anything but an ordinary paragraph or a scene break: a quote, a list, a table, an image, a heading, a paragraph with a footnote or code, the drop cap paragraph) must be followed by a block on the same page, since a later page might mean it ran onto it. A chapter with such a block at a page foot, or any mismatch, keeps the estimate whole (never half Typst and half estimate). `paginate` takes them as `forced` breaks and simulates nothing; the plugin places a break inside a paragraph at the word's document position (an inline spacer in the middle of a line when the webview wraps differently) and gives `paginate` the webview's line it falls on as `linesBefore`, only for the heights of the spacers and cards. A card grows only when a page holds more than a line over its text box (the webview's wrapping can differ from Typst's by a line). The report in use is the last one even while edits make it stale, as for the first page number: typing inside a paragraph keeps the breaks where they were until the next measure (2 s after the last edit); adding or removing a block, or changing its kind, fails the structure check at once and falls back to the estimate until the next measure. The cursor's page on an exact layout counts the spacers at or before the caret (they move with the text), and the status bar drops the "≈" from it only when that layout is exact and the report is fresh (`usePageCount.exact` and `usePageMeasure.fresh`).

**Rejected:**

- **Block-level marks only.** Wrong wherever a paragraph straddles a page: the paragraph's text would be put on one page whole.
- **A mark per line (`par.line` numbering).** Gives each line's page without the boundary rules above, but no text offset: the lines could not be mapped back to a ProseMirror position, and a line mark per line of a book is more tags than word marks per page break.
- **Marks inside words** (so that no mark is ever at a line break). They split the shaped text, which changes kerning and with it, rarely, a line break: the measured layout would no longer be the PDF's.
- **End marks and a tag after the last word** (`#bm-e()`): moved to the next page at a foot (see above), so they report a page too far.
- **Requiring the report to be of this very text** (a hash of the chapter): every keystroke would drop the breaks to the estimate and back two seconds later, and the cards would jump a page or more each time.
- **Splitting only at the webview's own line starts.** The text on a page would not be the PDF's text; the spacer is put at Typst's word instead, mid-line when needed.

**Limits:** see "Exact page breaks, part two" below for what changed the next day; the rest still holds: the editor's wrapping inside a page can differ from Typst's by a line (the webview's and Typst's line breaking are different engines, so this is inherent and not something to remove: the page a line is on is Typst's, the line wrap inside that page is the webview's), and breaks and the cursor's page are only as exact as the last measure after a change that moved text across a page.

## Exact page breaks, part two (2026-10-29)

**Decision:** the marks now cover every kind of content, so a chapter no longer falls back to the estimate because of a quote, list, table, image, heading, footnote, inline code or a block at a page foot; Read mode, front and back matter use them; a word Typst hyphenates across a page break begins the next page at the right character; a chapter is measured on its own, soon after it was opened or edited. The rule that a chapter never mixes exact and estimated breaks is unchanged: any mismatch or a break that cannot be drawn keeps the estimate for the whole chapter.

**Leaves, not blocks.** The exporter marks _leaves_: a paragraph, heading, definition term, scene break, image or block of code. A quote, list or table is marked by its leaves (`markedBlocks` sets `ctx.mark` per top-level block and `block()` marks every leaf as it writes it, so nesting needs no second walk). Every leaf now has a known end (its last split's page, else its start page; scene breaks and images never run over), which removes the old "next block on the same page" rule and the `q`/`o` kinds. Kinds: `p`, `h`, `s`, `i`, `k`. Text leaves get a `#wm(w)` mark per word (headings too); a block of code gets one mark per line through `show raw.line` scoped around that block (`#cm`/`#wm`/`bm-line` are defined only when marking); the drop cap paragraph is marked inside `#dropcap[...]` (the package takes the paragraph apart and sets it again, and the marks survive: tested with a drop cap paragraph that runs over pages). Footnotes are an inline item of the size ProseMirror gives them and belong to no word (`wordStarts` takes a number item for them); inline code is split into one `#raw` per word so a mark can sit between words (monospace, so the seam changes nothing). `parsePageReport` numbers the leaves of a block (`BlockPages.leaf`). A table's header row is repeated by Typst on every page, and so would be its marks, so it carries none, and the editor leaves it out of its leaves (`docLeaves`). Checked with real Typst for quotes, lists (nested, task), tables, definition lists, a multi-line heading, code blocks, images, footnote and code paragraphs, and matter: marks change nothing on any page (page text compared), and every leaf, split and line is where `pdftotext` finds its words.

**Editor.** `exactBreaks` pairs the report's leaves with the document's (`docLeaves`, blank paragraphs the exporter skips are left out) and returns breaks as (block, leaf, word, char). `forcedBreaks` (plugin) draws them: a break before the first leaf of a block is the old block-level spacer; one before a later leaf goes between items (`pageStartAt` climbs to the outermost node that starts with the leaf: a list item, a table row, the nested list) as a block spacer in the flow; one inside a leaf is an inline spacer at the word. Both carry `PageBreak.at` = `{ y, flow }` (the y in the block read from the DOM) because a quote, list or table has no line grid of its own; `layoutPages` uses it for the heights, `linesBefore` is then only the break's number in its block. A page that begins in the middle of a table row was first left to the estimate; it is drawn per cell since "Exact page breaks, part three".

**Where the marks come from.** The book's measure (status bar total, first page numbers) no longer carries marks: they cost about a second per 100,000 words (1.9 s against 0.7 s on a 100,000-word chapter; almost all of it is the 100,000 tags in the layout, the report loop is 0.2 s and inlining the call saves 0.1 s, neither a clear win). Marks are asked for only where breaks are drawn: `measureChapter` typesets just the open page entry (`gather(only)` reads one page), 500 ms after an edit and at once when a chapter is opened, so the breaks are exact within a fraction of a second instead of after the book's 2 s wait plus a book-sized run (the layout inside a chapter does not depend on the pages before it); Read mode asks for the book with marks (`useMeasuredPages(paged, true)`), once, since a reader has no edits. `breaksReport` picks the newer of the chapter's own and a book report that has marks for the entry; `freshReport` lets the cursor's page be exact from the chapter's measure. This is also the answer for the first measure and for chapters whose blocks changed: the first measure is the chapter's own (no wait), and an edit that adds or removes a block shows the estimate for the time of one chapter measure.

**Hyphenated words.** The word marks sit between words, and Typst puts a mark at a line break on the line before it, so a page that begins in the middle of a word (hyphenated, or broken after a hyphen) looks the same as one that begins at the word. `measurePages` with marks therefore runs a second query where only the words some page began with are marked, between every two characters (no word marks, about the cost of an unmarked run); the first mark on the page's page gives the character (`splits[].char`). The second run is trusted only when the book has the same page count and every entry the same pages, otherwise (or on any failure) the report keeps the words. The editor places the spacer at word start + char and the existing hyphen widget draws the hyphen. Checked with real Typst: the page before ends with the head and a hyphen, the page begins with the tail, for every hyphenated break of a test book.

**Preview.** Zoom to fit (the pages of the spread across the pane, recomputed for each spread and window size), Ctrl or Cmd with the wheel, gesture events (Safari and so the macOS webview turn a trackpad pinch into them) and two fingers on a touch screen (`lib/export/previewZoom.ts`; the zoom is continuous, the buttons go to the next step from where it is, 25% to 400%). A click on a page finds the word under it: pdf.js gives text runs, split at spaces with the run's width shared out by characters (`wordAtPoint`); `alignWord` walks the page's words and the chapter's words from where the page begins together (running head, folio, a repeated table header and footnote text are skipped, a hyphenated word or a drop cap matches its pieces) and the editor puts the caret there; a click on no word, the head, or a page that does not line up opens the start of the page as before. Right-to-left text is handled since "Exact page breaks, part three" (pdf.js lists a right-to-left line's runs from its left edge, not in reading order).

**Rejected:**

- **Marking every leaf with an end mark.** Moved to the next page at a foot, as before.
- **One mark per line from `par.line`.** No offset in the text; counting words in the line body is not reliable.
- **Marks inside every word** (to find hyphenation without a second run): they split shaped text, which can change a line break; only the second run, checked against the first, does it, and only for the words that matter.
- **Hyphenating in the editor with the same patterns:** needs Typst's hyphenation dictionaries in the webview.
- **A faster 100,000-word marked measure:** inlined `wm` (-0.1 s), a cheaper report loop (-0.2 s), per-paragraph marks plus a refinement run (no gain, two runs). Not marking the book unless asked for is the clear win.
- **Keeping the last report's alignment through an edit by diffing blocks:** the chapter's own measure answers within a second and is exact, a diff would be a second source of truth.
- **Drawing a page that begins inside a table row with one spacer:** a spacer inside a cell stretches the whole row (the other cells would not move). Done with a spacer per cell, see part three.

**Limits:** the marks need a Typst run per chapter (a 100,000-word chapter takes about 1.2 s marked, 2.5 s with the second run); right after a block is added or removed the estimate shows for about a second; a table row that Typst splits across pages and the word under the click on a right-to-left page were limits here and are closed in part three; Page view for Hebrew and Arabic stays left-to-right only.

## Exact page breaks, part three: table rows split across pages, and the click on a right-to-left page (2026-10-29)

**Decision:** a table row that Typst cuts at a page foot is drawn exactly, so a chapter with one no longer keeps the estimate; and the print preview's jump to the word under the click works on right-to-left pages (Hebrew, Arabic). The all-or-nothing rule is unchanged: marks that do not fit (below) keep the estimate for the whole chapter.

**What Typst does with a row** (checked with the real binary by `marks.rows.render.test.ts`, against the text of every page): the cells of a row are laid out side by side from the row's top on the row's page, and each cell is cut where its own text reaches the foot of the page, so every cell that is long enough continues at the top of the next page, one that ends sooner does not, and the row ends where its tallest part does. A cell never skips a page; the first paragraph of every cell starts on the row's page; a later paragraph of a cell starts on the page its cell has reached or the next (so a cut can fall between two paragraphs of a cell as well as inside one). The marks reported this in document order, cell by cell, so leaf n+1 (the next cell) can be on an earlier page than leaf n.

**Drawing it** (a spacer per cell, not one per page break). `ExactLeaf.cell` (`docLeaves`) numbers the row and cell of every leaf in a body row of a table; `exactBreaks` takes the leaves of one row together (`rowBreaks`): the row starts a page (`rowStart`, a break before the row, also when its first cell is blank) or continues the one before, each cell's paragraphs follow one another from the row's page, and for every page the row runs onto one `ExactBreak` carries `cuts`, the place in each cell that runs onto it (a word, with the hyphenation character as before, or the start of a paragraph of the cell). A cell that starts later than the row, skips a page, or goes back keeps the estimate. `forcedBreaks` (`rowBreak` in the plugin) reads each cut's place from the DOM (`splitY` for a word, `flowY` for a paragraph, both relative to the table) and makes one `PageBreak` per page with `at.cuts`; `layoutPages` sizes the spacer of each cut by where that cell was cut (`page height + gap - the text above the cut on its page`, the page's start for a cell's first cut, its earlier cut for a later one), `buildDecorations` puts a spacer widget at each (an inline one at a word, a block one between paragraphs of the cell), and every cell's next lines start at the top of the next card while the row is as tall as its tallest cell. What follows the row must sit where the cell that runs longest puts it, so the page's end in the flow (the `y` the next page is counted from) is the lowest cut for a page in the middle of a row and, for the last page of the row, the cut of the cell with the longest rest less what the other cells add to the row's height; with that the cards stay one page pitch apart below the table (`withTops` still corrects from the DOM). The sideways shift of left-hand pages (`pv-verso`) is per cell too (`pieces`: the text of each cell by the page it is on; a cell that ends before the cut is on the row's first page), and the cursor's page (`exactCursorPage`) counts a cell's own spacers inside it, and all of the row's after it. The header row is repeated by Typst and has no marks, so a table with one is handled as before.

**The click on a right-to-left page.** pdf.js gives a right-to-left line as a run per shaped piece in the order the PDF draws them (left to right on the page) with the text of each piece in logical order and a `dir`. `wordAtPoint` puts the runs of each line (consecutive runs with one baseline) in reading order, from the right edge when the line has a right-to-left run (a number or a Latin word in the line stays whole in its place, and its words inside it keep their order), and counts the characters of a right-to-left run from its right edge for the word under the point. Arabic words that have a ligature glyph (lam-alef) come out of pdf.js with letters in another order than the text's, so `alignWord` compares the letters of a word of a right-to-left script as a set (and takes pieces of a drop cap as a sub-multiset). Checked with real Typst and pdf.js (`pdfWords.render.test.ts`: every word of a Hebrew and an Arabic page, with a number and a Latin word in the line, maps to its word of the text) and in the preview with a real Hebrew PDF (`e2e/preview-extras.spec.ts`, desktop and phone width). It also fixed a left-to-right fault found on the way: the room below a run's baseline that still counts as on it was a third of its size, which for a large drop cap reached the lines beside and under it; it is capped at 4 pt.

**Rejected:**

- **A spacer inside only one cell, or a break before the row that moves** (a row that does not fit at the foot goes whole to the next page): the page would not have the PDF's words, and a row Typst splits would show on the wrong page. This is what Typst does not do.
- **Padding the cell's own content or a CSS `break-before` on the cell:** a pad is a height and so has the same effect as a spacer, with more machinery; the editor has no CSS fragmentation (the pages are one tall column).
- **Making the PDF's rows unbreakable** (`breakable: false`) so the editor never has to cut one: it changes the printed book (a tall row would leave a gap or overflow), the editor must show the PDF, not the other way round.
- **One spacer per break with the cell cuts averaged:** cells are cut at different lines (line grids differ by a wrap), so the next lines of a cell would be off the card's top by the difference.
- **Re-reading pdf.js text with `getTextContent({ disableNormalization })` or a bidi library for right-to-left order:** the runs are already in logical order, only the order of runs in a line is wrong; sorting by position is a few lines and needs nothing new.
- **Matching a right-to-left page by position only** (the nearest word of the text by place on the page): the editor has no place on the page; the walk of words is what aligns.

**Limits:** Page view for Hebrew and Arabic books is still left-to-right (the table's cells, the drop cap and the binding); a cell that ends in the editor lower than Typst ended it by a line can reach below the page cut (the same line-wrap difference as everywhere in Page view).

## Print preview: zoom and open a page in the editor (2026-10-29)

**Decision:** the preview's toolbar has zoom steps (50, 75, 100, 125, 150, 200, 300 %, kept while the preview is open; pages are drawn at `50 px/in x zoom` and redrawn sharp at each step), and a click on a page of the real (Typst) preview opens the chapter in the editor at the text that starts that page. The pages are buttons ("Open page N in the editor"); an "Edit this page" pill shows on hover or focus, and always on touch screens.

**How:** `previewTarget` (`state/project/output/preview.ts`) asks Typst for the layout of the same run as the preview's PDF (same cover, bleed, crop marks and trim size, `measurePages` with `blockMarks`) the first time a page is clicked, keeps it for the last preview key, and `lib/export/previewTarget.ts` finds the first block that starts on the physical page, or the word a paragraph runs onto it at, else the block the page is inside of; a page no entry owns (the cover, a blank verso) gets the entry after it. The preview then sets `state/pages/previewJump.ts`, selects the chapter, switches to Write mode and goes back to the book; the chapter's editor takes the jump on mount (`takePreviewJump`), puts the caret there and scrolls it into view, in Page view or not.

**Rejected:**

- **A layout run with every preview.** It would double the cost of opening the preview for a click most openings never make; the click pays one layout instead (about as long as the preview itself, cached after).
- **Reading the chapter from the PDF** (its outline or its text through pdf.js): ambiguous for repeated titles and gives no position inside the chapter.
- **A jump to the click's position through a glyph to source map:** Typst gives none; done later by matching the words of the page with the chapter's (see "Exact page breaks, part two").
- **Hover-only affordance:** no hover on a tablet or phone.

## Read mode: continuous and paged (2026-10-28)

**Decision:** Read mode shows the whole book in one vertical scroller, front matter, chapters and back matter in reading order, instead of one chapter at a time. At tablet width and up (`md`, `useIsMd`) it always draws the book as trim-size pages: cards, mirrored margins, running heads, page numbers, the chapter opening (ornament, label, drop cap), reusing the Page view stack. On a phone it keeps the plain reading layout (`.prose-reading`, text size and width from the reading settings) and also scrolls through the whole book. Page view stays a Write-mode preference: it is not offered in Read mode and Read ignores it. The reading settings keep their menu and become the zoom of the pages in page format (the size steps scale the fit-width zoom by 0.8, 0.9, 1, 1.12 and 1.25; "wide page" leaves a 16 px gutter instead of 48 px); on a phone they keep their old meaning.

**How it works** (`editor/reading/ReadingBook.tsx`, `editor/reading/ReadingSection.tsx`, `lib/reading/readingBook.ts`, `state/view/readingPosition.ts`):

- `ReadingBook` reads every page once with `readPages` when the reader opens (that flushes the Write-mode text first) and renders one read-only TipTap editor per page, each inside its own `<section>`. The app keys it on the book, edition and external reloads, not on the open chapter, so following the scroll never remounts it. The `Pagination` extension is loaded for these read-only editors (it only decorates); `PageFrame.tsx` was split into `usePageLook` (geometry and print font, once per reader), `PageStack` (cards, text column and zoom of one page) and the Write-mode `PageFrame` around it.
- **Page numbers are chained top to bottom.** A chapter's first page is the previous chapter's first page plus its laid-out page count (reported by its editor's pagination, a blank verso it draws first included), else the word-count estimate, so a chapter's number is settled by the chapters above it. Typst's printed number for the chapter (`reportStartNumber`; `useMeasuredPages` is mounted while the reader is shown) replaces the chain when there is a usable report, and the blank verso before it is drawn when it is one page past the chain. Front and back matter are numbered from 1 and take no part in the chain, as in Page view. The pure rule is `chainFirstPages`.
- **Only chapters near the viewport have an editor.** An `IntersectionObserver` on the scroller (900 px above and below) mounts a section's editor; the rest are placeholders of the right height: the height remembered from when the laid-out chapter was last on screen, else an estimate (`pagedHeight`: pages x (page height + gap) x zoom from the word count or the measured count; `plainHeight` from words, text size and column width). A newly mounted chapter keeps its placeholder height until its pages are laid out, so mounting moves nothing. A 40-chapter, 60,000-word book keeps 8 or fewer editors mounted (`e2e/reading.spec.ts`).
- **The scroll stays put while heights change.** WebKit (the macOS webview) has no scroll anchoring, so the reader sets `overflow-anchor: none` and does it by hand: it remembers the section at the top edge and its offset, and when any section changes size puts that section back at the same offset (and does nothing if it did not move, so a repeated notification cannot double-correct).
- **Position follows the scroll.** The chapter at a reading line 30% down the viewport (the last one when scrolled to the end) and, in page format, the page printed there, go to `state/view/readingPosition.ts` and the footer ("Chapter 2 of 3 · Page 14"). A quarter of a second after the reader stops on another chapter, `currentId` follows through `selectChapter` (so the chapter list, Write mode and Details agree); that echo does not scroll again. A chapter chosen elsewhere (chapter list, Previous and Next) is scrolled to, to its opening page rather than the blank page before it, and the scroll is repeated as that chapter lays out until the reader scrolls on their own. Entering Read mode from Write starts at the open chapter.
- CSS: `.prose-reading` only applies to the plain layout (`.prose-reading:not(.prose-page)`) and the stack's `.prose-page` never shares an element with it.

**Rejected:**

- **One merged document for the whole book.** The pagination plugin and the `> h1:first-child` CSS (chapter opening, drop cap) assume one chapter per document, a 400,000-word document would paginate in one pass and re-lay-out everything on any change, and the exporters' contract is one document per page.
- **One editor per chapter with Previous and Next only (what Read mode was).** No continuous scroll, no page numbers across chapters, and nothing to build a page-aware reader on.
- **A Typst-rendered reader (PDF in the webview).** Exact breaks, but it needs a PDF viewer dependency and a Typst binary on every machine, a compile of the whole book for every change of size or text, and it would replace the reader's text size and theme with a picture of a page. Typst is still used for the page numbers.
- **Mounting every chapter.** A 100-chapter book would hold 100 editors and paginate them all at open.
- **Browser scroll anchoring for the height changes.** Chromium has it, WebKit does not, and the app runs in WebKit on macOS.

**Limits:** the chained numbers are estimates until the chapters above have been on screen or Typst has measured the book, so a long jump into a book can show numbers that change by a page or two as the chapters above lay out; breaks inside a chapter are Typst's, as in Page view (see "Exact page breaks, part two").

## Find and a remembered position in Read mode (2026-11-06)

**Decision:** Read mode has a find bar, and remembers where each book was last read across sessions.

**Find** (`editor/reading/ReadFindBar.tsx`, `useReadFind.ts`, `lib/search/readFind.ts`, `state/view/readFind.ts`):

- Cmd/Ctrl+F in Read mode opens a bar on the same `useFind` store as Write mode (query, Match case, Whole word, Regex), docked like the Write bar. There is no replace row and no "All chapters" chip: Read mode is read-only and always searches the whole book.
- **Counts come from the pages' JSON, not from editors.** `ReadingBook` already holds every page, so `readMatches` runs `searchBook` over them (the same matcher as the Write bar's book scope, so the numbers agree with what an editor highlights) after a 120 ms pause in typing. A match is addressed by its number in the book (`index`, 0-based, reading order); `locate` turns it into a chapter and the match number inside it. "3 of 12" and the per-chapter list (`FindResults`, shared with Write) are therefore right for chapters that have no editor.
- **Highlighting is the Write search plugin.** Each read-only chapter editor loads `Search` and gets the applied search as a prop; the chapter that holds the current match marks it with the new `goToMatch` (current only: no selection, no scroll). Editors that mount later (scrolling, a jump) pick the search up when they are created.
- **Jumping reuses the reader's jump.** `useChapterJumps` gained `goTo(id, goal)` and `align` takes a goal, which can now also be a place in the chapter (a fraction) or the current match, beside the existing heading. A jump to a chapter far away scrolls to its placeholder, which brings it near the viewport, mounts its editor, which marks its match and reports it; each report and each layout of the chapter repeats the scroll, now to the element of the current match (40% down the viewport), until the reader scrolls on their own or 2.5 s pass. The open chapter follows (`selectChapter`), so the chapter list and the footer agree. A new search starts at the first match on or after the chapter at the reading line, wrapping.
- The query is not saved across sessions, as in Write mode.

**Remembered position** (`state/view/readingMemory.ts`): the chapter at the reading line and how far down it the line is (0 to 1; a fraction survives zoom, width and font changes, a page number does not) are kept in `localStorage` (`cadabracraft.readingPositions`), keyed by the book's folder like the recent books list (an app preference, never in the project files, which hold no path). Writes wait 400 ms for the scrolling to pause and are flushed when Read mode closes; at most 50 books are kept (oldest read dropped); a damaged value is ignored. Read mode resumes there the first time a book is read in a session, only if the open chapter is still the one the book opened with (a chapter chosen in Write mode or the chapter list wins, as before) and the chapter still exists. While a jump is under way the position is not saved (the scroll is not the reader's yet).

**Rejected:**

- **A search plugin on one merged read-only editor, or asking each mounted editor for its matches.** Chapters without an editor would not be counted and a jump to them would have nothing to ask.
- **Mounting every chapter to search it.** The reason chapters are virtualised.
- **Saving the printed page number as the position.** It changes with the template, trim size and font, and is only an estimate until the chapters above are laid out.
- **Storing the position in the manifest.** It is per reader and per computer, and would change the synced project every time someone scrolls.
- **A Tauri store for the position.** The other preferences are in `localStorage` and a new plugin is not worth one small value (see "Preferences stay in `localStorage`").

**Limits:** a match inside a chapter whose page breaks Typst has not reported yet may move a little as the chapters above settle (the jump corrects itself for 2.5 s); the bar has no pen frame yet (see roadmap).

## Node 26 LTS (2026-10-28)

**Decision:** development, CI and the MCP bundle run on Node 26 (26.13.0 "Lithium", the LTS line, supported to April 2029). Pinned by `.nvmrc` (`26`), `engines.node: ">=26"` in the root `package.json`, and `node-version: 26` in the CI, release and Pages workflows (the major, so CI follows LTS patch releases). Node is a dev and CI dependency only: the app is Tauri/Rust and bundles no Node.

**Why:** Node 26 is the current LTS line, and `@types/node` was already on 26. Nothing in the repo uses an API removed in 25 or 26 (`fs.F_OK`, `--experimental-transform-types`, `_stream_*`, `module.register`). On 26.13.0 the unit tests, typecheck, Playwright e2e, Rust tests, the MCP bundle build and stdio smoke run, and the real-app scenario give the same results as on 24.

**Rejected:**

| Alternative                                           | Why not                                                               |
| ----------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Stay on Node 24                                       | Works, but ends its LTS sooner and leaves types ahead of the runtime. |
| Node 25 (Current)                                     | Odd releases never become LTS and are unsupported after a few months. |
| Keep the MCP bundle documented for "Node 24 or newer" | Only 26 is tested, so `docs/mcp.md` says Node 26.                     |

**Follow-ups / caveats:** since Node 25 the official Linux binaries link against libatomic, which a runner image may need installed; the PR run on ubuntu will show whether it is missing. The scenario's "PDF export produces a 6x9 PDF" step fails on Node 24 and 26 alike (a poppler "Suspects object" message), so it is not caused by this change.

## Sidecar timeout kills the process tree (2026-10-28)

**Decision:** `platform/sidecar.rs` starts each sidecar in its own process group on Unix (`CommandExt::process_group(0)`) and, on timeout or cancel, sends SIGKILL to the whole group before reaping the direct child with `wait`. On Windows it runs `taskkill /T /F /PID`. Normal exit, non-zero exit and error mapping are unchanged. Tests spawn a shell with a background `sleep` and assert the grandchild is gone after a timeout and after a cancel.

**Why:** `Child::kill` only signals the direct child, so a wrapper script or a sidecar that spawns ffmpeg left the grandchild running (and holding CPU, files and the output folder).

**Rejected:**

| Alternative                             | Why not                                                                                                           |
| --------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Add the `libc` crate for `killpg`       | Not a direct dependency; one `extern "C"` declaration of `kill`, already linked by std, is enough.                |
| Spawn the `kill` command                | One more process per kill, and a missing or different `kill` binary on a stripped system.                         |
| A Windows job object (`windows-sys`)    | A new dependency and unsafe surface for a rare path; `taskkill /T /F` ships with Windows. Not run on Windows yet. |
| Kill only the direct child (status quo) | Leaves grandchildren running.                                                                                     |

## Moves never replace; directories are fsynced (2026-10-28)

**Decision:** trash, restore and reference-trash (Rust `move_to_free_name`, TypeScript `trashFile`) move a file with `rename_no_replace`: `fs::hard_link(from, to)` (atomic, fails with `AlreadyExists` when `to` exists, on every OS), then remove `from` (if that fails, `to` is removed again so the page never has two names). When the name was taken between the exists check and the move, the next free name (`-2`, `-3`...) is used, at most 64 times. Importing a reference uses `tempfile`'s `persist_noclobber` (the same link-then-unlink) and skips a name that appeared meanwhile. Where the filesystem has no hard links (exFAT, some cloud folders; any non-`AlreadyExists` link error) it falls back to an exists check plus `rename`, which leaves a small race window but is no worse than before. The parent directory is fsynced after every atomic-write rename and every move (`sync_dir` / `syncDir`; both directories for a move). That fsync is best effort and its error is ignored: the rename already happened and the data was fsynced, so a filesystem that cannot fsync a directory must not fail a save; on Windows a directory cannot be opened, so it does nothing there.

**Rejected:**

| Alternative                                               | Why not                                                                                                          |
| --------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `renameat2(RENAME_NOREPLACE)` / `renamex_np(RENAME_EXCL)` | Per-OS code, and needs the `libc` crate or unsafe FFI (no new dependencies); Windows has no equivalent in `std`. |
| Keep exists check then `rename`                           | Racy, and `rename` silently replaces on a lost race (always on Windows).                                         |
| Exclusive `create_new` of the target, then rename over it | Leaves an empty file if the process dies between the two, and `rename` over it is still a replace.               |
| Fail the save when the directory fsync fails              | Breaks saving on filesystems that refuse it, for a durability gain the author cannot act on.                     |

**Limits:** `atomic_write` itself still replaces its target by design (that is a save); snapshot names (`write_copy`) are chosen by an exists check and written with `atomic_write`, so a simultaneous second writer of the same snapshot name could replace it (open in the roadmap).

## Pickers run in Rust; only picked paths are accepted (2026-10-28)

**Decision:** the pickers are the Rust commands `pick_folder`, `pick_image`, `pick_import_source` and, added later, `pick_reference_files` and `pick_font_file` (`commands/picked.rs`, dialog calls in `platform/native_dialog.rs` through `tauri_plugin_dialog`'s blocking API on the blocking pool). Each remembers the chosen path, canonicalised, in the managed `PickedPaths` state under a kind (`Folder`, `Image`, `ImportSource`, and later `Reference` and `Font`) and returns the path as chosen to the webview, so the UI is unchanged. `create_project` accepts a root only if it was picked as a `Folder`, `import_asset` a source only if picked as an `Image`, and `read_import_source` and `read_import_image` (its `source_file`) only a path picked as an `ImportSource`; the argument is canonicalised first, so a link or another spelling of a picked path matches and a child, sibling or link to something else does not. Anything else fails with `ProjectError::NotPicked { name }` (file name only, never the absolute path; translated in `projectError.ts`). A pick **stays valid for the session**, bounded to the 32 most recent per kind (oldest dropped, a repeated pick refreshes). Opening an existing project and Recents are unchanged: they need a real `cadabra.json` already. (The reference and font pickers first stayed in the webview; they now run in Rust too, and `import_reference` accepts only a path picked or dropped as `Reference`, `fonts_import` only one picked as `Font`.) In dev-automation builds the pickers pop the `DialogQueue` themselves (`automation::take_queued`), so queued answers are recorded like real picks.

**Why session-valid and not one-shot:** a failed `create_project` (folder already holds a book, disk full) or `import_asset` (image too large) is retried without opening the dialog again, and an import reads the source once and then each Markdown image against the same pick, so one-shot would need a re-pick or a second pick kind. The bound plus the kind check keep the reachable set to what the author deliberately chose, which is the point: a compromised webview can no longer name an arbitrary folder to write a project into or an arbitrary file to read, only the few the author already picked.

**Drops are recorded in Rust:** a file dropped on the window is recorded as a `Reference` pick from the operating system's native drag-drop event (`commands::record_drop`, folders and missing paths skipped), never from a path string the webview sends. Webview-supplied paths for `import_reference` and `fonts_import` are refused with `not_picked` unless they were picked or dropped this way. `import_asset_bytes` still takes bytes the webview already holds, not a path. The rest of the boundary (command allow-list, caps, untrusted input) is in "Rust hardening: IPC boundary, memory caps, untrusted input (2026-10-29)" below.

**Rejected:**

| Alternative                                               | Why not                                                                                                                                 |
| --------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| One-shot picks (consumed on use)                          | A retry after a recoverable error, and an import's source-then-images reads, would need a new dialog each time.                         |
| Return an opaque token instead of the path                | Larger UI change (Home shows the folder, Recents and import report use the path) for the same guarantee, since the path check is exact. |
| Treat any child of a picked folder as picked              | Lets a webview create a project (and so write files) anywhere below a broad pick such as the home folder.                               |
| Keep the webview dialog and have it "register" the path   | The webview is the thing not trusted; it could register any path.                                                                       |
| Gate on a path prefix allowlist (home, documents) instead | Still lets a webview reach everything under it, and breaks authors who keep books on other volumes.                                     |

**Limits:** a file replaced between the pick and the use is not detected (canonicalisation happens at use, not at pick time). The OS dialog calls are not unit tested (OS UI, excluded in `scripts/coverage.mjs`); the recording and checking are.

## Third-party notices and license checks (2026-10-29)

**Decision:** the installer ships `LICENSE`, `NOTICE`, `LICENSE-CLARIFICATIONS.md` and generated notices in its `licenses/` resource folder, and Help > Licenses… shows them. A CI job (`licenses` in `ci.yml`, `pnpm licenses:check`) fails on a license outside the allowlist, on a RustSec advisory, and on notices that are out of date.

- **Rust:** `cargo-deny` (`apps/desktop/src-tauri/deny.toml`) enforces the license allowlist, bans wildcard versions, allows only crates.io, and checks advisories. `cargo-about` (`about.toml`, `about.hbs`) writes `licenses/THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES-rust.txt` for the four release targets, without dev and build dependencies. The advisory ignores in `deny.toml` each carry a reason: `zip` 4.6.1 comes from `tauri-plugin-updater` and only unpacks an archive already verified against our update key; the unmaintained GTK and zbus crates sit under Tauri's Linux stack.
- **JavaScript:** `scripts/npm-licenses.mjs` walks the production dependency tree of `apps/desktop` (`pnpm list --prod`), skips build-time branches (`@tailwindcss/vite`, the Lingui macro plugin) and per-platform native builds (so the output is the same on every OS), checks each license against an allowlist (an `OR` expression passes if one term is allowed) and writes `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES-npm.txt` with identical license texts merged. It lists a few more packages than the bundle strictly holds (for example `@napi-rs/canvas`, an optional Node-only dependency of `pdfjs-dist`); over-listing is the safe side.
- **Programs and fonts:** the hand-written `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.txt` still covers Typst, Inter, sherpa-onnx, ONNX Runtime, the audio encoders and the LGPL FFmpeg (source offer, hash, configure options and how to swap the program); see "M4B audiobook (ffmpeg sidecar)".
- **Help > Licenses…:** a `read_license` command takes one of six fixed ids and reads the file from the resource folder; the webview never sends a path. Dev builds get the same folder from Tauri's resource copy.
- **Rejected:** a license list written by hand (it drifts), shipping only a link to the repository (the notices must travel with the binary), and allowing GPL or LGPL crates (the Commons Clause license cannot ship them; the FFmpeg exception is a separate program under LGPL, not linked).
- **Allowed with care:** MPL-2.0 (file-level copyleft, unmodified dependencies, source public). The npm `lightningcss` (MPL-2.0) is build-only and not shipped.
- **Not done:** the license review by a lawyer, the trademark search, and signing; see `docs/roadmap.md`.

## Task lists and definition lists (2026-10-29)

- **Decision:** five schema nodes in `packages/editor-schema`: `taskList` > `taskItem` (boolean `checked` attr, content `paragraph block*`) and `definitionList` > `(definitionTerm+ definitionDescription+)+` (term: inline content; description: `paragraph block*`). No `formatVersion` bump and no migration: like tables, they are additive, and an older app refuses a chapter that uses them. `chapterDocProblem` refuses a non-boolean `checked`. Own nodes instead of the `@tiptap/extension-list` task extensions: no new dependency, and the HTML form (`ul[data-type=taskList]`, `li[data-type=taskItem][data-checked]`) stays under our control. The editor flips a box on mouse down on the checkbox label (one undo step); the list commands are menu-only (Format > Task List, Insert > Definition List), so no toolbar frame is needed yet.
- **Mappings:** Markdown `- [ ]` / `- [x]` and Pandoc `Term` + `: description` (both ways); HTML `dl` and checkbox `li`; DOCX checkbox content control (`w14:checkbox`, read and written) and "Definition Term" / "Definition" styles; EPUB `ul.task-list` with a disabled checkbox and `dl`; PDF an `enum` whose numbering draws a ticked or empty box, and bold terms with indented descriptions; audio speaks terms and descriptions as paragraphs and does not announce box state; MCP plain text `- [ ]` / `: text`. Importers split a list that mixes task and plain items instead of losing checkboxes; a description with no term before it stays plain blocks, and a trailing term becomes a paragraph, so imported content always fits the schema. Every exporter throws on a wrong child node or a non-boolean `checked`.
- **Rejected:** keeping the `[ ] ` text prefix (state is lost on round trips and it reads wrongly aloud and in print), a `checked` marker mark on list items (the box is a block property), and a Markdown form for descriptions holding blocks other than paragraphs (no form that reads back, so the Markdown export throws).

## Rust hardening: IPC boundary, memory caps, untrusted input (2026-10-29)

Triggered by a memory-safety and security audit of `apps/desktop/src-tauri`. The crate was already clean (one `unsafe`, no production `unwrap`, bounded reads, atomic writes); this records what changed and why.

**Lint policy.** `Cargo.toml` `[lints]`: `unsafe_code`, `clippy::unwrap_used`, `expect_used`, `panic` and `undocumented_unsafe_blocks` are `deny`; `clippy.toml` exempts tests. The one `unsafe` (`kill` in `platform/sidecar.rs`) carries an `#[allow]` and a SAFETY comment. `rust-version = "1.99"`; CI runs `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`.

| Rejected                                                 | Why not                                                                                                                                                               |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `clippy::indexing_slicing` and `as_conversions` as lints | 173 sites, mostly audio DSP on values bounded by construction; the parsers of untrusted input already use `get`/`checked_*`. Fuzzing and the perf harness cover them. |
| `rust-toolchain.toml` pin                                | CI deliberately tracks stable via a pinned action; a pin would fight it and force component downloads. `rust-version` states the floor instead.                       |
| `cargo audit` as a second job                            | `cargo deny` already checks advisories with justified ignores.                                                                                                        |

**Reference and font sources go through `PickedPaths`.** `import_reference` and `fonts_import` took any absolute path from the webview (a local-file-read primitive: copy a readable file into a project, read it back). They now accept only picked paths (`PickKind::Reference`, `Font`; commands `pick_reference_files`, `pick_font_file`); files dropped on the window are recorded in Rust from the OS drag-drop event (`commands::record_drop`), never from a path the webview sends. This replaces the "no pick-by-drop" note above. `Reference` keeps 512 picks, other kinds 32. Extensionless references are refused unless a signature sniff or a plain-text check passes (an ELF/Mach-O/MZ header or `#!` is refused); `open_reference` refuses them outright; the `BLOCKED` extension list now covers about 100 launchable types.

| Rejected                                       | Why not                                                               |
| ---------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| A native dialog on every drop                  | Breaks drag-and-drop.                                                 |
| Webview `open()` reports the pick back to Rust | The webview is the untrusted side; it could report any path.          |
| Allowlist of openable reference types          | Stops authors opening their own `docx`, `xlsx`, `zip` research files. |
| Refuse every extensionless file                | Breaks `README`-style notes.                                          |

**Formatting.** `apps/desktop/src-tauri/rustfmt.toml` (`max_width = 160`, `use_small_heuristics = "Max"`) matches the width the crate was written in (about 300 diffs against it, 1,300 on defaults); the crate was formatted in one formatting-only commit and CI runs `cargo fmt --check`. The `BLOCKED` extension table keeps its rows with `#[rustfmt::skip]` (one name per line would add 80 lines). Run `cargo fmt` in `apps/desktop/src-tauri` before committing Rust.

**Navigation guard, no per-command ACL.** A `navigation-guard` plugin allows only `tauri://localhost`, `http://tauri.localhost` and (debug builds) the dev server. Not done: `on_new_window` (needs the main window built in `setup` with `create: false`; a popup gets no IPC capability and the CSP sets `frame-src 'none'`), and a Tauri `AppManifest` per-command ACL (it would generate an `allow-<command>` permission for each of the 67 commands that `capabilities/default.json` must then list; a wrong list fails at run time in a way the mock-runtime `ipc_tests` cannot see, so it needs a real-app run with `/drive-app` and was left out).

**Drift test (built).** `src/ipc_tests/acl.rs` reads the real files (`lib.rs`, `capabilities/default.json`, `tauri.conf.json`): every command in the handler list is known to the real handler and is in the `every_command()` argument table (or on a short, checked excuse list), the table has no stale names, the capability covers exactly the `main` window (the one configured), every plugin permission belongs to a plugin `lib.rs` registers, none is a wildcard, and `opener:allow-open-url` stays scoped to GitHub addresses. Adding a command without updating the table, or a permission for a plugin that is not registered, fails `cargo test`. Still open: the per-command `AppManifest` itself.

**Sidecars.** Release builds run only the bundled sherpa and ffmpeg (as Typst already did); `SHERPA_TTS_BIN`, `FFMPEG_BIN` and PATH apply under `debug_assertions` only. `typst_query` rejects selectors that are empty or start with `-` and passes `--` before positionals. Rejected: a runtime flag for the override, which could be flipped in a release build.

**Raw bodies for binary IPC.** `import_asset_bytes` takes a raw request body with `root` and `name` as percent-encoded headers (`commands/wire.rs`); the JSON number-array form of both it and `write_project_bytes` is gone (a 25 MB image became a JSON array several times larger before any size check). Rejected: base64 in JSON (still a 33% copy plus a `String`). `write_project_file` contents are capped at 32 MB and `create_project`'s manifest at 1 MB and must be a JSON object (`ProjectError::InvalidManifest`).

**Snapshots.** A whole-book snapshot file is at most 32 MB (the read cap), measured on the serialized file; a snapshot that can be created can always be restored. Each file carries a `summary` written before the texts, so listing reads the first 256 KB and takes the size from metadata (old files stream through the head fields). Retention keeps the author's count and size (default 50 snapshots and 1 GB; see "Book snapshot limits, pinning and rename"), moving the oldest to `.cadabra/trash/<stamp>-snapshot__<name>` (never deleting; `list_trash` does not show them, recovery is by hand). Rejected: raising the read cap to 256 MB (a 256 MB `String` plus parse per restore), a separate index file (drifts from the files), bumping `FORMAT` (orphans old files), hard delete (CLAUDE.md).

**Other caps.** `write_wav` and `parse_wav` use checked arithmetic and reject rates above 768 kHz; the M4B copy into the project is capped at 2 GiB (streamed in chunks with `project::write_bytes_from_file`; the file is never held in memory, and the cap is re-checked while copying) and a batch WAV from the engine at 512 MB; `download_file` re-hashes an existing same-size file, stops after 30 minutes per file (the `.part` is kept for resume) and creates `.part` exclusively unless resuming; `AudioJobs` holds at most 256 cancel flags (unheld ones are evicted; if all are held the caller gets an unstored flag) and `MediaRoots` at most 64 tokens. Mutexes in `media.rs` and `picked.rs` tolerate poisoning.

**`.docx` import limits (Rust).** Declared sizes are checked before inflating: 64 MB for `word/document.xml`, 16 MB for any other XML part, 200 MB and 10,000 entries in total (unchanged). The XML tree has one budget per import shared by every part: 2,000,000 elements, 4,000,000 attributes, 64 MB of decoded text, 256 attributes per element; over any of them the import fails with `too-many-nodes` (its own message, because "too large" is wrong for a small file that expands into a huge tree). The cap is 2M, not 5M: a tree costs about 150 bytes per element in release and about 700 in debug, so 2M is roughly 300 MB at worst. `import_docx_owned` (the app entry point) drops the file bytes once the archive is read and each XML part's bytes once parsed. Measured peak RSS did not change in a debug run (the DOM dominates, about 590 MB for 55 MB of XML); the caps, not the freeing, bound the worst case. Frame header and image lengths use `u32::try_from`; over 4 GiB is an error. These per-part caps are Rust-only (the TypeScript importer has none).

| Rejected                                     | Why not                                                                                   |
| -------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Reusing `archive-too-large` for node floods  | Wrong wording for a small archive.                                                        |
| Per-part element caps                        | N parts multiply the bound.                                                               |
| Streaming the XML to output instead of a DOM | A rewrite of the importer.                                                                |
| Changing `import_docx` to take ownership     | Every test and the contract runner would clone; the owned variant is the app entry point. |

**Limits.** TOCTOU between path resolution and open remains (no `openat`/`O_NOFOLLOW`); only a local attacker racing the app can use it. The Windows Rust job has never run.

## One writing surface: the formatted book (2026-10-30)

**Decision:** the editor always shows the book as it will be printed. At `md` width and up, Write mode is the page stack that was "Page view" (template type, trim-size pages, running heads, page numbers, Typst's breaks when its marks fit). On a phone the same template type, indents, headings and scene breaks are drawn reflowed at the full screen width (`ReflowFrame`, `.prose-reflow`), with no page edges, running heads or page breaks. The Page view header button, the View > Page View menu command (and Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+P), and the Settings rows for the manuscript font and the writing column width are gone; the stored `pageView`, `manuscriptFont` and `writingWidth` values are ignored and dropped on the next save. Only the page zoom (Fit, 75%, 100%, 125%) remains a preference.

**Why:** authors saw their text change shape between the plain editor, Page view, the print preview and the export (font, width, leading, justification, indents, scene breaks, chapter openings), which read as inconsistency. One surface removes the mode to forget about, and the pen already drew it (`docs/design.md`, "Writing surface").

**Rejected:**

- **Keep a plain "Draft" mode next to the formatted one:** it keeps the inconsistency available and doubles the editor styling and tests.
- **A scaled-down trim-size page on a phone:** the most faithful, but the text ends up too small to write in.
- **Let writers hide the page edges:** the screen would stop matching the book again; the zoom already controls how much page is visible.

**Follow-ups / caveats:** the template's print font is used only when it is installed, bundled or imported, else the bundled serif (roadmap: bundle Libertinus Serif). The phone reflow uses the template's text size (11 pt for the novel), which is smaller than the old 17 px manuscript text. Read mode and the print preview are unchanged. Not verified on a real phone.

## Accessibility targets and tooling (2026-10-30)

**Decision:** the app is held to WCAG 2.2 AA, its EPUBs to EPUB Accessibility 1.1 and its print PDFs to PDF/UA-1. Enforcement is `eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y` in `pnpm lint`, an `@axe-core/playwright` spec in the existing e2e run, and a Vitest check of the design-token contrast ratios. `Modal` remembers the element that opened it and gives focus back on close, because dialogs are controlled and have no Radix Trigger. Details and open work: `docs/accessibility.md`.

**Why:** the code already had good habits but nothing stopped a regression; the first axe run found that closing any dialog dropped focus to the page body.

**Rejected:**

- **Claiming a conformance level:** no independent evaluation exists, and the EPUB `conformsTo` decision (omit it) still holds.
- **A paid or hosted scanner (Lighthouse CI, VertaaUX):** needs accounts or network calls, against the v1 rules.
- **Running axe only in CI as a separate job:** the Playwright suite already has the fake backend and fixtures; a spec there runs locally and in CI with no new wiring.

**Caveats:** automated tools find roughly a third of WCAG issues; screen-reader passes are still manual. The `no-autofocus` lint rule is off because dialogs and inline editors move focus on purpose.

## Language of parts: the `lang` mark (2026-10-30)

**Decision:** a passage in another language than the book's (WCAG 3.1.2) is a mark, `lang`, with one attr `lang` holding a BCP 47 tag. It is additive (no `formatVersion` bump, like `decorative`): `chapterDocProblem` refuses a missing, non-string, malformed (anything but letters, digits and single hyphens) or over-35-character tag, and `isLangTag` in `@cadabracraft/shared` is the one definition every exporter checks again before writing the tag. The mark is not inclusive, so typing after the passage returns to the book's language, and a second `lang` mark on the same text replaces the first. The editor renders it as `<span lang="...">`; the app has a "Language of selection..." dialog (a pick from the enabled languages or any tag) and "Clear language" in the Format menu (menu only at first, not on the toolbar: one more button wraps the toolbar to two rows at the usual window width, which moves the editor and broke e2e specs that click its centre). Exporters: EPUB `<span lang xml:lang>`; PDF `#text(lang:, region:)` with the same language and region split as the book language (`langAndRegion` in shared); Word `w:lang`; RTF `\lang` from the existing Windows language table, nothing for a language outside it; Markdown `<span lang>` only with `htmlMarks` (the Markdown and HTML importers read it back as the mark, validated with `isLangTag`; an invalid tag stays literal text with the `html` warning), otherwise plain text (it is the one HTML mark that does not make the export throw, because losing a language hint loses no words). The audiobook exporter ignores it: the voice is the edition's, so a French phrase is read by the English voice.

**Rejected.** A block-level language attr (a foreign phrase is usually inside a sentence). Making the mark inclusive (the next word would silently be tagged French). Throwing in Markdown without `htmlMarks`, like underline (the author would have to opt into HTML to export a book that merely contains a French phrase). Per-passage voices in the audiobook (a different model per phrase; out of scope).

## Table of contents page (2026-11-01)

**Decision:** a book can have one Contents page, a new single-use front matter kind `contents` (format v6 to v7, `/change-file-format`). Its chapter file holds only a heading (`starter.ts`); the list is generated, never stored. The manifest entry may carry `toc`: depth 0 to 3, a page-number switch and overrides layered on the live outline (hidden keys, per-language titles, an optional order, custom entries). `packages/format/src/toc.ts` holds the one pure resolver (`resolveToc`, plus `attachPages`, `staleOverrides` and the edit helpers) that the editor, the exporters and the MCP tools share; `extractOutline` moved from `apps/desktop/src/lib` into `packages/format` so exporters can use it. Items are keyed by page id, or `entryId#level:normalizedText:nth` for a heading, so an override survives edits elsewhere in the chapter; one whose key matches nothing is reported as stale and kept until the author removes it. Page numbers come from the real layout (the Typst page report for PDF, the live report in the editor); EPUB and the text formats list titles only. The v7 bump is a pure version change so an older app refuses the project cleanly instead of failing on the unknown kind.

**Why:** the list must follow the book (a renamed or reordered chapter should update it) yet stay editable, which is the layered-override model; a pure resolver in `packages/format` keeps it testable at 100% and identical everywhere; no editor node keeps derived data out of chapter files and out of every exporter.

**Rejected:**

| Rejected                                                                | Why not                                                                                                                                                  |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| A `tableOfContents` TipTap node in the chapter                          | Touches `editor-schema`, the shared `DocNode` type, every exporter and the schema-contract test, and stores derived data in the chapter that goes stale. |
| An export-time-only setting with no page                                | Invisible while writing: no page in the sidebar, no place to edit entries, nothing in the print preview.                                                 |
| A frozen copy of the list written into the contents page (edit by hand) | Goes stale at the first chapter rename or move, and the author has to rebuild it; page numbers could not follow the layout.                              |
| Keying headings by their index in the chapter                           | Any heading added or removed above shifts every key and silently moves overrides onto the wrong heading; text plus occurrence survives unrelated edits.  |
| Dropping an override whose heading changed                              | Loses the author's work without telling them; stale overrides stay on disk, are listed, and are removed on request.                                      |
| Making the bump part of an additive change (no version bump)            | An older app would drop `toc` on save, or fail on the unknown kind; a version bump makes it refuse the project instead.                                  |

**Follow-ups / caveats:** heading page numbers in the editor need a marked measure (only the open chapter is measured). A Word TOC field, an EPUB page-list and tablet or mobile design frames are roadmap items. Typst convergence of a very long list was not tested in this phase.

**PDF (`export-pdf`):** `PdfOptions.toc` carries the resolved items; the Contents page is a matter page (recto, no folio) whose list is one Typst `context` that finds each entry through the `<chapter-start>` marks (pages, by id) and new `<toc-heading>` marks (headings, by item key, written inside the heading by outline position, only for headings in the items) and prints the footer's number (`counter(page).at`), so Typst's repeated layout settles the roman numbers when the list grows. Numbers and dot leaders drop when either `toc.pageNumbers` or `pageNumbers` is false. Checked with real Typst 0.15.1 (`toc.render.test.ts`): the printed numbers equal the page report's, a list that grows from one to three pages moves the roman matter and settles, and a book with 300 entries compiles with no convergence warning, so reserving the page count is not needed. A title in a right-to-left script makes its whole line run right to left (number first, leaders between), which is what a right-to-left reader expects. Wrapped long titles use an explicit `par(hanging-indent: ...)`: a `set par` inside the entry's block gave wrapped lines no hanging indent in 0.15.

## Contents page: one shared render for the editor, Read mode and the exports (2026-11-03)

**Problem:** Read mode drew the Contents page by appending one paragraph per entry (title indented with em spaces, chapters bold) to the page's own text, inside the read-only editor. It had no page numbers or dot leaders, no links, no mirroring for a right-to-left book, none of the template's margins and type, and the template's big first letter was applied to the first entry. The editor's preview, the PDF and the other exports each had their own copy of "hide the hidden items, fall back for a blank title, nest by level, bold the chapters".

**Decision:** the list is decided in one place and drawn in one place.

- **Entries (`packages/format/src/contents.ts`):** `contentsLines(items, numberOf?)` (visible items in order, with the printed title, level, bold and number), `contentsTitle` (blank title falls back to the entry id, then "(untitled)") and `nestContents` (nesting by level). The PDF (`tocEntries`), EPUB (in-book list and navigation), Markdown, Word and Rich Text all take their entries and titles from these, instead of each filtering and nesting by hand; `visibleTocItems` became generic so exporters' own item types pass through it. `export-pdf` and `export-markdown` now depend on `@cadabracraft/format` (it only depends on `shared`, so no cycle). The numbers stay the caller's: Typst's own `counter(page)` in the PDF, none in EPUB and the text formats, the report or estimate in the app.
- **Drawing (`apps/desktop/src/app/toc/ContentsSheets.tsx`):** `ContentsSheets` (the page at the trim size, template margins and type, dot leaders, numbers, mirrored when the book is right to left, a long list continuing on further cards) and `ContentsEntries` (the list itself, also the phone layout). `useContentsView(entry)` produces everything they need (rows with Typst or estimated numbers, printed lines, layout, title, direction) and is used by the Contents editor (its list and its approximate preview) and by Read mode (`editor/reading/ReadingContents.tsx`, which `ReadingSection` renders for a front-matter page of kind `contents` in place of the text editor). In Read mode each entry with a page is a link (`<a href="#id">`, handled in script) that calls the reader's own scroll-to-chapter machinery (`open` in `ReadingBook`: it selects the chapter, aligns to its opening page, or to the heading by its position among the chapter's headings once the chapter is laid out). The list is inside a `nav` named "Contents entries"; the leaders are decoration (`aria-hidden`).
- **Removed:** `withContentsList` (`lib/export/tocExport.ts`), the em-space paragraphs.

**Why:** a change to what a Contents page lists (a rule for hidden items, a blank title, the level of a custom entry) is made once and every output follows; Read mode shows the page the author edits and the PDF prints, including numbers (the report's when Typst measured the book, "≈" estimates otherwise, none when the switch is off).

**Rejected:**

| Rejected                                                                            | Why not                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Keeping the paragraphs in the read-only editor and adding numbers and links to them | A tab or leader cannot be written in the editor schema (it is the exporters' contract), a link to a heading would need a node or mark only this page uses, and the paginator would still split the list on its own. |
| Rendering the PDF's Typst pages in Read mode                                        | Typst is optional, the pages are pictures (no links, no live text), and the reader would wait on a compile for every edit; the approximation is the same drawing the editor falls back to.                          |
| One component that exporters also use (HTML, Typst, OOXML from one tree)            | Each output format has its own markup and numbers; what they share is the data (`contentsLines`, `nestContents`), which is pure and lives in `packages/format`. `packages/*` must not import from the desktop app.  |
| Putting `contentsLines` in `apps/desktop/lib`                                       | The exporters could not use it.                                                                                                                                                                                     |

**Follow-ups / caveats:** the PDF still prints with Typst's code (`tocDefs`), so the look is kept in step with `ContentsSheets` by hand (the approximation is already only close). On a phone, where Read mode draws no pages, nothing measures the book for the Contents list, so its numbers are estimates until a measure exists. A link to a heading in the plain (phone) layout lands on the chapter's top if the chapter's editor is not yet mounted.

## Toolbar overflow menu: More formatting (2026-11-03)

**Decision:** the formatting toolbar ends with one "More formatting" button (an ellipsis, `MoreFormatting.tsx`, a Radix dropdown built from `ui/Menu.tsx`). It holds Strikethrough, Code, Superscript and Subscript (checkbox items, `aria-checked`), Language of selection... and Clear language (plain items; Clear is disabled outside a language passage), and Image details, Scene break, Code Block, Task list and Definition list.
