- **Which pages.** `pageIsCopyright(section, kind)` (the `copyright` kind, never a chapter) and `pageIsNarrow(centred, section, kind)` (a centred matter page, never a chapter and never the title page, which has its own full-width layout) in `apps/desktop/src/editor/centring.ts`. `PageStack` takes the entry's `kind` and puts `page-copyright` or `page-narrow` on the page; `PageFrame` passes `entry?.kind` and `ReadingSection` passes `page.kind`.
- **Numbers.** `pageCssVars` adds three properties from the template and the trim: `--page-imprint-size` (0.85 × the body size, the PDF's `set text(size: 0.85em)`), `--page-imprint-space` (0.7 em − the template's leading, the same net-of-leading convention as `--page-para-space`, so the gap between paragraph baselines matches Typst's `par.spacing`), and `--page-narrow-inset` (11 % of the text width on each side, so the block is 78 %). Constants `IMPRINT_SIZE_EM`, `IMPRINT_SPACING_EM`, `CENTRED_BLOCK` sit beside `DROP_CAP_LINES`.
- **The copyright page** (`.prose-page.page-copyright`, in `index.css`) sets the editor's type at the imprint size, gives the paragraphs no indent, ragged lines and the imprint gap, and puts the whole page against the bottom margin. The first block gets `margin-top: auto` in a flex column, which is empty space on a short page and nothing on a full one; a blank page before it is a page-break spacer, so the auto margin goes on the first block after it.
- **A centred page** (`.prose-page.page-narrow`) insets the editor's padding on both sides and centres its paragraphs' lines. Headings keep the template's alignment (`--page-h1-align` and the rest), as the PDF's heading rules set their own alignment over `align(center)`, and a paragraph with the author's own `data-text-align` still wins over both.
- **Both passes.** The block width and the imprint's size are set in the measuring pass (`.pv-natural`) as well as the drawing pass: line wrapping and line heights have to be measured at the size and width the page draws, or the breaks would not match the page after the block is applied. Only the foot alignment waits for the drawing pass: the measuring pass must see the imprint flow from the top margin, because with the offset the page's free space would read as content and the pagination would push the page onto a second card. The justification for the selectors is in the comments beside them.
- **The block follows the choice, not the cards.** A centred page that grows past one page keeps the 78 % block and loses only the vertical centring (`centredPage`), as in the PDF, where the wrapper stays and the aligned block simply overflows.

### Why

The page view is what the author judges a page by, so a page that the print PDF sets one way and the app draws another is a page the author is writing blind. These were the last two of the three differences a page-by-page comparison of a real book found (see "PDF fidelity: blank lines, the drop cap, the chapter opening"): the copyright page is mostly empty space with one small block at its foot, and a centred dedication or epigraph is a narrow centred block, not full-width text in the middle of the page.

### Rejected alternatives

| Rejected                                                                 | Why not                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `text-align: center` on the whole editor of a narrow page                | It would also centre a heading the template sets left (the PDF's heading rules keep their own alignment inside the block), and it would reach into list items and quotes rather than the block. Centring the paragraphs is what the PDF's `align(center)` over `par(justify: false)` amounts to.     |
| Set the block's width in the drawing pass only                           | The pagination measures the full-width lines, the block is applied afterwards, every line rewraps, and the breaks no longer match the page drawn. The width has to be in both passes.                                                                                                                |
| Bottom-align the imprint in the measuring pass too                       | The empty space above the imprint would be measured as part of the page, so the imprint would paginate as an overflowing page and the stack would grow a second card for text that fits. The measuring pass reads the page from the top margin; only the drawn page moves it down.                   |
| `justify-content: flex-end` instead of an auto margin on the first block | A page whose imprint is taller than the text box keeps its foot on the bottom margin and pushes its first lines above the top one, where the card clips them. The auto margin gives up the free space first, so an over-long imprint starts at the top margin and runs on like any overflowing text. |
| Absolutely position the imprint over the card                            | It would leave the editor's flow: the pagination could not measure it, the caret could not reach it, and the page would stop being one editable document.                                                                                                                                            |
| Take the EPUB's 0.4 em imprint gap                                       | The EPUB has no page and spaces the imprint for reflow; Page view draws the printed page, so it takes the print's 0.7 em wherever the two disagree. The 0.85 em size and the flush paragraphs are the same in both.                                                                                  |
| A per-page option for either layout                                      | Both are what the kind and the template mean, like the chapter ornament. The manifest already refuses `verticalCenter` on the contents and copyright pages because they have a layout of their own; a switch would contradict that.                                                                  |

### Follow-ups / caveats

- The running head on a matter page's continuation pages, the last of the three, is drawn as well (see "Running heads on matter pages' continuation pages" below).
- An imprint taller than its text box is drawn from the top margin and clipped at the card's foot rather than above the top margin; the foot alignment has nothing to give up at that point. The pagination handles it as any long matter page.
- No pen change: the pen does not draw front or back matter pages in Page view (see "Front and back matter (not drawn in the pen)" in design.md), so `docs/design.md` only gains a sentence in the Page view "As built" note. No string changes, so no i18n work.
- Checked by Playwright: `pageview.spec.ts` (the imprint's size against the body's, its flush paragraphs and gap, and its last line on the bottom margin) and `vertical-position.spec.ts` (a centred page's 78 % width and centred lines, a title page full width, Default removing the block, and a page that grows keeping it). Unit tests: `pageCentred.test.ts` for both rules, `pageLayout.test.ts` for the three properties.

## Running heads on matter pages' continuation pages (2026-11-04)

### Decision

Page view and Read mode head a front or back matter page's continuation pages as the PDF does: the book title on the left-hand pages, the page's own title on the right-hand ones, and nothing on the page the entry opens on. Before this, matter pages had no running head at all — the last of the three print layouts Page view approximated (see "PDF fidelity: blank lines, the drop cap, the chapter opening" and the section above).

- **Which entries.** `matterHeadsOn(centred, section, kind)` in `apps/desktop/src/editor/centring.ts`: a chapter is never asked (it heads its pages by its own rule, the opening card being `open`); the title page, the copyright page behind it and the contents page print no head (the PDF's `folio-off` pages), and neither does any centred page (`header-off`, so a dedication, an epigraph, or a page the author centred).
- **Which cards.** A matter entry's opening card prints none: the PDF's header skips the page where `chapter-start` sits, so a one-page matter page is always bare and only the pages it runs onto are headed. `PageStack` finds the first card that is not a blank verso and asks `runningHead` for a head on the cards after it (`headsOn && i !== firstTextCard`). The card kinds `open` and `blank` already print none.
- **The title.** The entry's own title for the open edition, from the manifest — the same string the PDF puts in `state("chapter-title")` with `chapter-start(title, none, …)` when it starts a matter page. A page whose document heading differs from its list title therefore prints the list title in both surfaces.
- **Code.** `runningHead` in `apps/desktop/src/editor/pagination/frame.ts` keeps its `headed` flag, which `PageStack` now asks per card; its fourth parameter is named for the entry rather than the chapter, because a matter page uses it too.

### Why

The PDF's header rule is per page, not per entry, and a matter page that runs onto three or four pages read as one undifferentiated block in the preview while the printed book gave the reader the book title and the page title. Since a page view exists to show the page the author will print, the last surface difference in that list had to go.

### Rejected alternatives

| Rejected                                                                    | Why not                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Head the matter entry's first page too                                      | The printed book does not: the header is suppressed on the page a chapter or matter page starts on, where the title is either in the heading or deliberately absent. A preview that added one would push the author towards a head the PDF will not print.                                                                                       |
| Mark a matter entry's first card `open` so the existing rule hides its head | `PageSpec.kind` says where a card sits in the pagination (the chapter's opening is drawn without the sink and ornament and is treated as the entry's start everywhere), and a matter page's first card is a body card there. Turning it `open` would answer a header question with a layout kind and quietly change other readers of that field. |
| A per-page "running head" switch                                            | The PDF has no such choice, and the rule follows from the kind and the author's centring choice, not from a new decision per page. Nothing asked for it.                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Draw the head in Page view but not in Read mode (or the reverse)            | Both draw the same page through `PageStack`; a page that differs between the two is the same bug as a page that differs from the print.                                                                                                                                                                                                          |

### Follow-ups / caveats

- With this, all three print layouts that Page view approximated match the PDF; the roadmap line for them is gone.
- The head's type and position are the template's `runningText` (size and italics) in the top margin, as for chapters, so a template with small caps or italics heads sets matter pages the same way.
- No pen change (the pen draws no matter pages in Page view); `docs/design.md` only gains a sentence in the Page view "As built" note. No string changes, so no i18n work.
- Checked by Playwright: the new `pageview-pages.spec.ts` case (a foreword long enough for several pages: no head on its first card, then the book title and page title by side, and all heads gone once the page is centred) and the copyright case in `pageview.spec.ts` (no head on a `folio-off` page). Unit tests: `runningHead.test.ts` covers both rules, every branch.

## Blank pages the author adds (2026-11-04)

### Decision

A blank page is now a page of the book, not only something the printing rules insert. **Add page → Blank page** (or **Insert → Blank page**) adds one straight after the page that is open — front matter, between two chapters or the back matter — and it is a row in the list like any other page: you can move it, drag it, or delete it.

- **Manifest:** `blank` is a front and back matter kind (`packages/shared/src/matter.ts`) and the one kind a chapter entry may carry, so a blank page also sits between two chapters (`validateManifest`). `addBlank` in `packages/format/src/edit.ts` puts it in the section of the page it follows, at the end of the chapters when no page is open. `chaptersOf(manifest)` is the book's chapters without those blanks: chapter numbers (`chapterNumberOf`, `ReadingBook`), the Chapters count (Details, the Home dashboard), the "Chapter 3" title of a new chapter, translation progress and the audiobook all use it, so a blank page is never numbered, narrated or counted as a chapter. The entry keeps a file like every other page, holding an empty document, so reading, saving, translating, trashing and book undo need no special case. The document is never editable: `Editor` takes a `readOnly` prop and `App` sets it when the open page is blank, so no text can be typed into a page the PDF prints empty.
- **One layout rule:** `layoutBook` in `packages/shared/src/pageNumbering.ts` treats a blank page as already being the blank page, wherever it is: it never restarts the arabic count before the first chapter with text: it follows the page before it (never pushed to a right-hand page, never given a blank page of its own), it opens with no chapter opening (`OWN_OPENING`) and it prints no number (`NO_FOLIO`). Every surface reads that function, so the page cards, the status bar and the print preview agree.
- **PDF (Typst):** `bodyOf` in `packages/export-pdf/src/typst/matter.ts` writes `#page(header: none, footer: none)[]` for it and skips the page-break rules: no header, no number, no title, no bookmark. The page after it still sees the kind before the blank, so the rules around it (a dedication's blank verso, for instance) are unchanged.
- **Page view and Read mode:** the page is drawn as an empty card with no folio and no running head (`HEADLESS_KINDS`, `OWN_LAYOUT_KINDS` in `apps/desktop/src/editor/centring.ts`), and because it has its own row it is visible where the author put it instead of only as a gap in the numbering.
- **Exports that have no fixed pages:** the contents page leaves blank pages out (`TOC_UNLISTED_KINDS`), the EPUB and Markdown exports drop them, Word and Rich Text skip them, and `isTranslated` treats them as translated so a translation edition never asks for a translation of a page with no text.
- **Strings:** the page is called "Blank page" in the Add page menu, the Insert menu and the chapter list (`useKindLabels`, `menuModel`), and `starterTitle("blank", language)` names it in the book's own language.

### Why

An author sometimes needs a page that is empty on purpose — a breathing space after a quotation, a leaf between two parts, the back of a plate. The rules could only ever insert one blank page, in front of a page that had to open on a right-hand one, so two empty pages between two pages were impossible. Making the blank page a page of its own needed no new control, uses the list the author already reorders, and lets the printed book, the page cards and the reader show the same page.

### Rejected alternatives

| Rejected                                                        | Why not                                                                                                                                                                               |
| --------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| A "blank pages before this page" count on the neighbouring page | The pages would be invisible in the list, and a count fights the right-hand-page rule: one extra blank before a page that would have opened on the right puts it on the left instead. |
| An empty "Custom page"                                          | It prints a page number, takes text, appears in the contents page and in the EPUB, and an author would have to remember not to type in it.                                            |
| A book-wide "blank page" toggle                                 | The pages are placed one by one, between two particular pages; a book-wide setting says nothing about where they go.                                                                  |

### Follow-ups / caveats

- Checked with unit tests: `packages/shared/src/__tests__/pageNumbering.test.ts` (a blank page follows on, prints no number, and is not pushed even after a dedication), `packages/format/src/__tests__/matter.test.ts` (added straight after the open page, an empty document, and always "translated" for exports) and `packages/export-pdf/src/__tests__/matter.test.ts` (the empty page source and no page entry of its own). A blank page between two chapters has its own cases: it is inserted into `manifest.chapters` after the page it follows and is not a chapter for `chaptersOf` (format), it follows the chapter before it with no folio and the next chapter still opens on a right-hand page while the count is not restarted on the blank (shared), and Typst prints it as a blank page with no chapter opening for the chapter after it (export-pdf).
- A translation edition still gets an (empty) file for the blank page when the edition is added, because every entry does; it is never asked for.

## Parts: a divider entry in the chapter list (2026-11-05)

**Decision:** a part is an ordinary entry in `manifest.chapters` with `kind: "part"` (format 11), not a separate `parts` array and not a parent object around its chapters. Membership is positional: every entry after a part, up to the next part, belongs to it; entries before the first part are ungrouped. The divider's file is `manuscript/part-NNN-slug.json`, an ordinary chapter document whose Heading 1 is the page's printed title, while the manifest `titles` is the sidebar label. `chaptersOf` skips parts, so chapter numbering, word counts and all chapter id/file-position logic are unchanged. `partGroups`, `addPart`, `addPartBefore`, `movePart` and `removePart` in `@cadabracraft/format` keep the grouping and the manifest consistent; the sidebar, Page view, Read mode and exports read the same list. **Add part** inserts after the open page; **Start part here** on a chapter's row menu inserts before it, so an existing chapter can begin a new part in one step.

**Why positional:** it needs no new manifest section, gives one reading order for free (a part cannot disagree with where its chapters are), and a hand-written or older manifest without parts is valid as it stands. A separate array or nested chapters would need a second order to keep in sync and would break the flat `EditionEntry` assumptions in lookup, TOC and every exporter. The divider is a real page, so Page view, reading and pagination see it in the same stream as chapters; there is no synthetic page in any renderer.

**Contents:** parts are rows of the generated contents list, shown at the top level. A part's chapters are nested one step under it, and their headings follow below the chapter. The nesting is a display `indent` computed from the same positional membership (`1` for a chapter, `1 + heading level` for its headings), so `level` keeps its 0-3 meaning, every item key is unchanged, and no parent id is stored. `contentsLines`, `nestContents` and each exporter use `indent` when present, else `level`, which is why the editor, preview, Read mode, PDF, EPUB, Word, RTF and Markdown agree. `TocSettings.parts` defaults to `true` and is written with the page's other settings. While it is off, part overrides are dormant rather than stale, and chapters stay flat because there is no parent row; hiding one part row does the same for its chapters, so they are never nested under an unrelated row. Switching it back restores both the rows and the nesting. A part page never contributes a second, heading-level row.

**Rejected:** changing `level` to 0-4 or storing a parent id per item to nest chapters (it would change the shared 0-3 heading contract and every override key for a display concern; `indent` derives the same nesting without touching the manifest); a part as front or back matter (it belongs in the chapters stream and in chapter order); a separate `parts` array with chapter membership ids (two orders to keep consistent); a drag model that moves a divider on its own (the Contents list moves its whole block, and the sidebar uses Move part up/down).

**Moving a part (built):** each resolved row carries the transient `part` id it belongs to; moving a part row moves that whole continuous block, a chapter, heading or custom entry can move only inside its block, and a top-level row dropped on a part snaps before or after the whole block. The stored `order` is still a flat list of keys, so renames, hidden flags, custom entries and existing saved orders keep working; the editor's Move up/down action targets the next or previous block for a part row, and the announcement names the block's real position.

**Tests:** format unit tests for add/move/remove/restore and adding a part before a chapter, chapter numbering, TOC inclusion and the derived `indent` (part row, chapters, headings, custom entries, parts off), and for moving a part with its chapters, headings and custom entries while a chapter stays in its part and a top-level row snaps around it; shared page-numbering tests (recto, no folio, own opening); PDF `#part-page` and regenerated golden, plus `indent` in the list; EPUB and Markdown nested-list tests under a part; Word and Rich Text indent tests; desktop store and Contents switch tests; Playwright for grouping, the part page, **Start part here**, moving, removing a part without its chapters, collapsing and expanding a part's chapters in the list, the **Include parts** switch, the nested indent and moving a part block on the Contents page; an axe screen for the part page and its sidebar group. **Not verified:** the pen (`cadabracraft.pen` was not available), native builds, and a full translated screen sweep with the new strings.

## Scenes: a node inside the chapter (2026-11-05)

A scene is a `scene` block node that wraps the blocks the author writes as one unit, carrying its planning notes as
attributes (`id`, `title`, `synopsis`, `pov`, `status`, a free `label` and a `target` in words). Chosen over a file per
scene: page breaks, snapshots, the Page view measure and the format's per-chapter history stay exactly as they are, and a
scene can be split or merged without touching the file tree. The cost is that the history point is still the chapter,
which is what it is today anyway.

Nothing about a scene prints. Every exporter passes the node's children through unchanged (Word, RTF, Markdown, EPUB, PDF,
and the audiobook's text), and a scene's `title` is a working name, never a heading. The node's `status` is one of
`outline`, `draft`, `revised` or `final`, or empty for a scene nobody has triaged yet; an empty note means the default
(`""` or `null`), so a file written before a note existed still opens.

Adding the node does **not** bump `formatVersion`: chapter nodes never do (see "Chapter files" in
[file-format.md](file-format.md)). An older app refuses a chapter that holds a scene, which is the same rule every other
node addition followed; there is no migration because there is nothing to migrate.

## Spellcheck: the platform dictionary (2026-11-04)

### Decision

Spellcheck uses the dictionary the platform already has, called from Rust. It is not a dictionary the app downloads, and it is not only the webview's own squiggles.

- **macOS:** `NSSpellChecker` (`objc2-app-kit`): misspellings, guesses, the available languages, and a per-document ignore list through `inSpellDocumentWithTag:`.
- **Linux:** `libhunspell` through explicit paths (`Hunspell_create(aff, dic)`), not `libenchant`. Enchant only accepts a language tag and searches its own directories, which is exactly what a bundled dictionary cannot use (see below).
- **Linux dictionaries ship in the installer.** The `.aff`/`.dic` pairs are Tauri resources, so they land in `usr/lib/<ProductName>/dicts/` inside the AppImage and are read through `resource_dir()`. The five Latin-script languages the UI ships (en, es, it, fr, de) cost about 9 MB; Hebrew and Arabic are opt-in downloads because they add about 15 MB and the Hebrew dictionary is AGPLv3+, which must not enter the installer. `Hunspell_add_dic` carries the book's names list.
- **Windows:** `ISpellChecker` (COM, `Win32_Globalization`). Deferred to the launch phase with the Windows build; no Windows code is written before then.
- **One command, one call:** a Tauri command checks a chapter in a single call (`spell_check(text, language, names)` returning the ranges and their suggestions), debounced on the text and mapped back onto TipTap ranges. Per-word IPC is rejected.
- **One layer of underlines:** the editor sets `spellcheck="false"` once our own marks are drawn, so the author never sees two squiggle layers.
- **A book's names never enter the system dictionary.** `learnWord` and `Add` write to the user's global personal dictionary, so "Aelthwyn" would follow them into their email and every other app. The per-book names list lives in the project and is passed as session ignores on every check.

### Why

- No download, so nothing to pin, verify or size-budget; the installer stays small and the rule to ask before downloading stays untouched.
- The author's machine already knows their language and the words they taught it, so suggestions agree with the rest of their system.
- macOS and Linux are the first release platforms, so two integrations cover 1.0, and Windows joins when its installer does.
- The names list is ours in every route, because it has to travel with the book to another machine or a collaborator, so the platform choice never changes that data.

### Rejected alternatives

| `libenchant-2` with bundled dictionaries | Enchant's Hunspell provider takes a language tag and searches `<ENCHANT_CONFIG_DIR>/hunspell` and `$XDG_DATA_DIRS/hunspell`; no API hands it a path. The env-var workaround is process-global and also redirects personal word lists. |

| Rejected                               | Why not                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| -------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Bundled `nspell` and hunspell data     | One code path everywhere and no OS variance, but a dictionary download per language (a new dependency and a download the owner must approve), size in the installer, and it ignores the words the author already taught their system. Kept as the fallback if Linux coverage disappoints. |
| The webview's spellcheck alone         | It works today (the squiggles already come from the OS dictionary) but it is a black box: no list of misspellings, no suggestions we can show, and no per-book names.                                                                                                                     |
| A proofing report with no inline marks | Cheapest, but the author hunts words in a list instead of seeing them in the text.                                                                                                                                                                                                        |

### Follow-ups / caveats

- **Bundling replaces the Linux coverage risk** rather than depending on the host. Hunspell and Enchant are both LGPL (Hunspell is LGPL/GPL/MPL, so elect LGPL) and may be redistributed with notices and the licence texts, which the `licenses/` resources and `Help > Licenses` already do for other components.
- **The dictionaries are not all permissive.** en_US is SCOWL/Ispell (BSD-like), fr_FR is MPL 2.0, es and ar are GPL/LGPL/MPL tri-licences, de_DE offers LGPL among its terms, **he_IL is AGPLv3+ and must stay out of the installer**, and the Italian dictionary states no licence in the files read, so confirm it before shipping it. Add each elected licence to `binaries/licenses/` and to `licenses:check`.
- **The `.so` is a build-time dependency.** `libhunspell` is linked normally, so linuxdeploy resolves it when the AppImage is built: the Linux CI job must install `libhunspell-dev`, and a missing library fails the whole bundle rather than the feature. The dictionaries themselves go through `bundle.resources`, which linuxdeploy never scans, so no `ldd` failure there.
- **A pure-Rust reader is the alternative** (`spellbook`): no `.so` at all and dictionaries as resources only, but it is alpha and its own README warns that complex compounding dictionaries (German) work less well. `zspell` keeps suggestions behind an unstable feature flag. Revisit if bundling the `.so` causes trouble.
- `Hunspell_spell` expects the dictionary's own encoding, so convert from UTF-8 with `Hunspell_get_dic_encoding` first; Enchant did that for us.
- `NSSpellChecker` is not documented as thread-safe: run it on the main thread or serialise calls, and prefer the batch API (`checkSpellingOfString:startingAt:…`) over a call per word.
- Windows needs COM initialisation and the `windows` crate feature before that work starts.
- Not verified: the suggestion quality and language coverage of each API, and the cost of checking a 20,000-word chapter in one call.

## The codex (2026-11-05)

A book's people, places, things and lore live in one file beside it, `codex.json`, not in the manifest and not in `.cadabra/`. The author opens it from **Codex** under **Book snapshots** in the chapter sidebar: a list filtered by kind, an editor for the selected entry, and the scenes that name the entry.

- **One file, not many.** A sidecar like `comments/`, so a copy, a sync client and a diff all see plain JSON. Nothing is exported: no exporter reads it, so adding it cannot change a made page.
- **Scenes point at entries by id, and the POV stays text.** A scene's `cast` holds codex ids; its `pov` stays free text, because a book written before it had a codex already has a POV, and a POV may be a phrase no entry carries. `appearsIn` counts a cast hit by id and a POV that is the entry's id, name or alias in any case, so old notes link up without a migration and without rewriting prose.
- **Ids and names are unique.** Two entries called "Mara" would make the cast picker and **Appears in** ambiguous; the file refuses a repeat, the way comments refuse an unknown key.
- **The whole file is written on every change.** The codex is small. A write that fails puts the previous list back and says so, and a file that is not a codex is refused rather than overwritten.
- **No `cast` picker yet.** The format and `appearsIn` take a scene's cast, and the panel reads it, but no scene UI writes one; today a scene's POV is what links it to an entry. The picker is drawn and built with the codex's pen frames.
- **`formatVersion` stays 10.** A new optional sidecar changes no manifest field, and the version describes the project's files as the app opens them. The bump belongs with the reading of `codex.json` in the versioned format; the attempt that raised it to 11 turned the migration suite red without changing behaviour, so it was backed out rather than rushed.

Alternatives rejected: keeping entries in the manifest (rewrites the book's index on every note), one file per entry (a folder to sync and keep consistent for no gain at this size), and a cast that stores names instead of ids (renaming an entry would silently unlink every scene that mentions it).

## Scene board: edits go through project actions, not the editor (2026-11-06)

**Decision:** the scene board (and the timeline after it) change scenes through two project actions in `state/project/scenes.ts`, `patchSceneIn(chapterId, sceneId, patch)` and `moveScene(sceneId, toChapterId, index)`, plus `wrapUnsortedIn(chapterId)` for loose text. Each one flushes typing in the open chapter, reads the chapter file(s) of the open edition, changes the TipTap JSON with the pure helpers in `lib/scenes/sceneMoves.ts` (`patchScene`, `removeScene`, `insertScene`, `reorderScenes`, `wrapUnsorted`), writes through `writePages` (backup, temp file and rename, with rollback of the files already written) and records one `docs` entry in the book's undo history, the same shape Replace all uses. If the open chapter was touched it is reloaded from disk afterwards. A cross-chapter move writes the destination first and the source second; if the second write fails and the first cannot be put back, the scene is in both files (a duplicate, never a loss) and Undo finishes the job. The editor's `useSceneEditingBridge` is unchanged and keeps serving the sidebar and the scene rail for the open chapter.

A story date is one more scene attribute, `date` (`YYYY-MM-DD`, optionally `THH:MM`; empty means none). Node attributes never bump `formatVersion`, so there is no migration; a chapter saved before `date` existed opens unchanged. Fantasy calendars are not modelled in v1 (roadmap).

Text outside every scene (a chapter written before scenes, or typed above the first one) shows on the board as an "Unsorted" card per chapter with its word count and a "Wrap into a scene" action. The chapter's leading title heading and empty paragraphs do not count as unsorted.

The pointer drag from `EntryList` moved into `ui/useDragReorder`, which knows nothing about the target's shape (a list index for the chapter list, a chapter and an index for the board). The keyboard alternative stays with each caller: arrow keys on the grip and a "Move earlier / later / to chapter" menu (WCAG 2.5.7).

**Why:** the open chapter lives in the editor, but a board has to edit chapters that are not open and move a scene between two chapters. Going through the files keeps one source of truth (the chapter JSON), reuses the save path (backups, conflict copies) and the undo stack, and keeps the editor unmounted while the board is shown.

| Rejected                                            | Why not                                                                                                                                      |
| --------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Opening each chapter in a hidden TipTap editor      | Slow (one editor per chapter), needs the DOM, and a move across two chapters would be two editors that must succeed or fail together.        |
| A separate scene sidecar file (`scenes.json`)       | Splits the source of truth: the synopsis and order would live apart from the text they describe and drift when the text is edited elsewhere. |
| A freeform canvas for the board                     | Much larger scope (layout model, zoom, undo) and no keyboard story; already rejected under "References", kept on the roadmap.                |
| Rewriting `EntryList` drag for the board separately | Two copies of the Escape, cancel and pointer handling to keep in step.                                                                       |

**Follow-ups / caveats:** each edit reads the book's chapters to find a scene, which is fine for a novel and not measured on 120,000 words. A duplicate scene id (left by a failed move) keeps the first card on the board and shows a notice; there is no automatic repair. Not verified against a book edited on two machines at once beyond the existing conflict-copy rule.

## Timeline: a view over the scenes, story order computed, date edits through the scene actions (2026-11-05)

**Decision:** the timeline (`app/timeline`) is a full-page view like the scene board and reads the same data (`useBoardData`, so the same chapter-file reader and re-read on every new file hash). The axis is computed by the pure `buildTimeline(board, order)` in `lib/scenes/timelineData.ts`. Book order is the board's chapters flattened. Story order sorts by each scene's `date` (`YYYY-MM-DD`, optionally `THH:MM`): the strings compare correctly as text, and a day without a time sorts before the same day with one. A scene with no date takes the key of the scene before it in the book, so it stays behind that scene and giving a few scenes dates never scatters the others; undated scenes at the start of the book sort before every dated one; ties keep book order (a stable sort on the book index). Nothing is stored for the order: the choice (Book order or Story date) is local to the view and resets when it closes. A date is edited on the row with a native date field and a native time field and saved through the existing `patchSceneIn(chapterId, sceneId, { date })`, on blur or Enter (a date typed digit by digit passes through other valid dates, so saving on every change would write many times); the value is checked with `isStoryDate` from `editor-schema` before it is written, clearing the day clears the time, and every change is one book undo step. After a change the new reading puts the focus back on the field that was edited, because the row may move. Scenes are not moved or reordered here (that is the board's job); no new file format, schema or project action was added.

**Why:** the board already solved reading chapters that are not open, writing through the save path and undo; a second reader or a second write path would have meant two sources of truth. Carrying the previous date forward is the smallest rule that is predictable and explainable in one sentence on the page, and needs no extra data. Native date and time inputs are real calendar controls with the platform's keyboard and screen reader support, and match the real-calendar rule in `docs/file-format.md`.

**Rejected:**

| Alternative                                            | Why not                                                                                                                        |
| ------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Undated scenes in a separate "No date" lane at the end | Loses where the scene belongs in the book, and a long book with few dates would be mostly one lane                             |
| Undated scenes sort first, or last                     | A scene sits far from its neighbours the moment one scene gets a date                                                          |
| Dragging scenes along the axis to set or change a date | Needs a date scale (zoom, gaps, ties) and a second drag model; dates are typed or picked                                       |
| A horizontal, zoomable axis with scaled spacing        | Fantasy calendars and big gaps in time make the scale arbitrary; a vertical list works at every width and with a screen reader |
| Saving the date on every change event                  | A year typed digit by digit writes the chapter file several times                                                              |
| Keeping the chosen order in the project file           | It is a way of looking, not part of the book; no format change                                                                 |

**Follow-ups / caveats:** an invented calendar is still not modelled (see roadmap). The order is not remembered between visits. Not built: grouping by day with a heading, a scale with real spacing between dates, filtering by POV or status, one lane per POV.

## Outline export (2026-11-04)

### Decision

The book's structure (chapters, their scenes, the scene fields, the cast as names) can be written as `outline.opml`, `outline.md` or `outline.csv` under `exports/<language>/`, by the new `packages/outline` (pure: `buildOutline` reads the chapter JSON, `toOpml`, `toMarkdown` and `toCsv` write, `fromOpml` and `fromMarkdown` parse for the import still to come). No chapter text is ever written. The app action is `exportOutline` (Export dialog, Outline format) and the MCP tool is `cadabra_export_outline`; both read `codex.json` only to turn cast ids into names, and fall back to the ids when it is missing or invalid.

### Why

- **OPML 2.0** is what outliners read, with the synopsis in the standard `_note` attribute and the scene fields in a namespaced `cc:` set a plain outliner ignores, so a round trip keeps them.
- **Plottr has no CSV import** (checked against its current help center: it imports Word, Scrivener and Snowflake Pro files). The plan's "CSV that Plottr can read" is therefore dropped; the CSV is a documented generic spreadsheet shape with fixed columns (`CSV_COLUMNS`).
- **CSV cells that start with `=`, `+`, `-`, `@`, tab or CR get a leading apostrophe**, so a synopsis cannot run as a spreadsheet formula. RFC 4180 quoting, CRLF and a BOM so Excel reads accents.
- **Parsers refuse hostile input**: a DOCTYPE, malformed XML, and files over `MAX_OUTLINE_BYTES`, `MAX_OUTLINE_CHAPTERS` or `MAX_OUTLINE_SCENES` raise `OutlineError`; bad values are cleaned with warnings.
- **Exporting needs no new binary or network call**; `fast-xml-parser` was already a dependency elsewhere.

### Rejected alternatives

- **A Plottr-specific CSV or a Word template**: no documented CSV import, and the Word import needs Plottr's own template; not worth guessing a format.
- **Writing OPML by string only without a parser**: the import needs one, and a shared test round-trips both.
- **Putting the outline in the manuscript Markdown export**: that export is the text; the outline is a different artifact for different tools.

### Follow-ups / caveats

- Import is built (S06b, below).

## Outline import (2026-11-05)

### Decision

An outline file (OPML, a CSV, or Markdown with `#` chapters and `##` scenes) is read into new chapters with scenes: `packages/outline` gains `fromCsv`, `parseOutline` (format in, `ParsedOutline` out, `OutlineError` for anything it cannot read) and `outlineToPages` (the parsed outline as chapter documents: a level 1 heading and one `scene` node per scene with title, synopsis, POV, status, label, target and story date, each holding an empty paragraph). The app reads it through the existing import wizard and the existing read-only `read_import_source` command, whose allowlist now also holds `opml` and `csv`; the parse runs in the webview (`parseOutlineSource`), and writing is the manuscript path unchanged (new chapter files, the manifest last, existing chapters never touched), for **Import…** (a new book) and **Import into this book**. The MCP server has `cadabra_import_outline` (adds to an existing book; `readSource(path, true)` in `project-fs` allows only the outline extensions). No Rust command was added and no network or binary is involved.

- **Scene ids are always new.** The file's own ids are read and validated by the parsers but dropped by `outlineToPages`: importing an outline into a book that already holds those scenes (or twice) must not create two scenes with one id, and the importer has no cheap way to know the book's ids (the wizard does not read the chapters). A re-import therefore adds a second copy instead of merging.
- **A Markdown file is ambiguous**, so the preview step has a **Read this file as** choice (Manuscript, the default and the old behaviour, or Outline). `.opml` and `.csv` are always outlines. The choice is made after the file is read, so it can be flipped without choosing the file again.
- **The cast is not imported.** OPML carries codex ids and Markdown and CSV carry names; the book's codex may not know either, and a wrong id would be a dangling reference. One `cast-skipped` warning says how many scenes named a cast.
- **Warnings reuse the Import report**: the outline warning codes become `outline-<code>` import warnings (severity warning), so the report, its copy button and the summary line need no new code. Errors are typed (`OutlineError.code`) and each has its own sentence in the app (`describeOutlineCode`) and a `details.importCode` in MCP.
- **A generic CSV reader**: RFC 4180 with a BOM, comma, semicolon or tab (picked from the header line), columns found by header name in any order with common synonyms, the apostrophe the writer adds before `=`, `+`, `-`, `@` taken off again, an unclosed quote refused (`malformed`) and a first row with no `chapter`, `title` or `scene` column refused (`not-csv`).
- **Limits** are the ones the parsers already had: 5 MB, 1,000 chapters, 20,000 scenes, no DOCTYPE or entities in OPML (so no entity expansion), each text cut to the schema's length with a warning.

### Why

- Reusing the wizard gives rename, skip, reorder and merge, the report, undo-safe appending and the same hardening for free, with one new control.
- Parsing in the webview keeps one parser (shared with MCP and tested at 100%) instead of a Rust copy, and the file is at most 5 MB of text, unlike a `.docx`.

### Rejected alternatives

| Alternative                                                   | Why not                                                                                                                                 |
| ------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| A separate Import outline dialog                              | A second copy of the pick, preview, plan, report and error handling; the wizard already has all of it.                                  |
| Keeping the file's scene ids when they are free               | The wizard does not know the book's scene ids, and a clash (two scenes with one id) is a worse failure than a copy.                     |
| Mapping cast names to the codex on import                     | Needs the codex read and a matching rule (case, aliases, duplicates); a wrong or missing match would be silent. Left for later.         |
| Deciding Markdown by sniffing (`##` headings and a `> ` line) | A manuscript with `##` headings is not an outline; a wrong guess would turn a book into empty scenes. The author chooses.               |
| A new Rust command for the parse                              | The input is small text and the parser already exists and is tested in TypeScript; Rust would only add a second parser to keep in step. |
| Accepting `.csv` in `cadabra_import_file`                     | That tool makes a new book from a manuscript and would read a spreadsheet as Markdown. The outline has its own tool.                    |

### Follow-ups / caveats

- Cast is dropped; a later change can map names to codex entries.
- Not verified in the packaged app (`pnpm tauri dev` or `/drive-app` did not run): the native picker's filter now lists `.opml` and `.csv`, and the Rust test only proves the extension allowlist.
- Plottr's CSV and Word templates and OmniOutliner's own `.ooutline` are not read; export OPML instead.

## Story-structure templates (2026-11-06)

**Decision:** the five structures (Three-act, Hero's Journey, Save the Cat, Romancing the Beat, Story Circle) are plain data in a new pure package, `packages/story-structures` (`STORY_STRUCTURES`: stages of beats, each beat a title and a one-or-two-sentence summary). `placeholderScenes(structure, newId)` turns one into scene nodes: the beat's title, its summary as the synopsis, its stage as the label, status `outline` and an empty paragraph as the body. The desktop app adds them with one more project action, `addScenesIn(chapterId, scenes)` (in `state/project/scenes.ts`, next to `patchSceneIn`, `moveScene` and `wrapUnsortedIn`), which reads the chapter file, appends the scenes after everything the chapter holds (`appendScenes` in `lib/scenes/sceneMoves.ts`, built on `insertScene`, so a repeated id throws) and writes through `writePages` as one book undo step. The scene board offers it from a `Story structure` button that opens a dialog (structure, chapter, a preview of the beats). Applying a template only adds scenes: nothing in the chapter changes, and Undo removes all of them at once. No file-format change: the scenes use the existing `scene` node and its notes, so there is no version bump.

**Why:** data keeps the structures testable and reusable (the MCP server or a later "new book from a structure" can read the same list) and out of the React code; a package follows the `templates` and `outline` pattern and carries the 100% coverage floor. Going through the same project-action path as the board's other edits keeps backups, conflict copies and undo identical. The summaries are written for this app, in our own words; the beat names follow common usage, since a framework's structure is an idea and not copyrightable.

| Rejected                                       | Why not                                                                                                                                                         |
| ---------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Putting the data in `packages/templates`       | That package is the typesetting templates (fonts, trim, drop caps); mixing in story data makes the export packages depend on text they never use.               |
| A `story-structures.json` read at run time     | A file to ship and parse for a list that is code-reviewed text; the package gives types and tests for free.                                                     |
| Creating chapters as well (one per act)        | The brief is scenes in the open book or chapter, and a chapter-creating action changes the manifest, the sidebar and the editions. Left for a later "new book". |
| Replacing or interleaving with existing scenes | Could overwrite or reorder the author's work. Appending is the only safe place, and the board's drag and keyboard moves put a scene wherever it belongs after.  |
| Translating the beat text through Lingui now   | About 120 strings times seven locales for text the author edits at once. The dialog chrome is translated; the beat text is written into the book, in English.   |

**Follow-ups / caveats:** the beat titles and summaries are English only, in every interface language (see roadmap). Adding to a chapter that has a translated edition adds the scenes to the open edition's file only, like every other scene edit. Not run on the native app (`/drive-app`).

## Code organization: size limits enforced by tooling (2026-11-05)

**Decision:** a source file is at most 400 non-blank lines, a test file at most 800, and a folder holds at most 20 source files. ESLint enforces file length for TypeScript (`max-lines` as an error, plus warnings for `max-lines-per-function`, `complexity`, `max-depth` and `max-params`); `scripts/check-size.mjs` (`pnpm check:size`) enforces Rust, CSS, test and folder limits. Both run in CI, in the `.githooks/pre-commit` hook (installed by the `prepare` script) and in `/quality-gates`. Files that already break a limit are grandfathered in `scripts/size-baseline.json` and in two temporary lists in `eslint.config.mjs`; entries may only shrink and a stale entry fails the check, so the lists drain to empty as files are split.

**Why:** `max-lines` was only a warning and skipped Rust, CSS and tests, so files grew past 1,000 lines and folders such as `src/lib` reached 63 files without any check failing. A hard gate with a shrink-only baseline lets the cleanup land in steps without letting new offenders in.

| Rejected                           | Why not                                                                                                              |
| ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Keeping `max-lines` as a warning   | Warnings were ignored for months; 8 TypeScript files sat over the cap.                                               |
| One cap for tests and source       | Test files group many cases per subject; 800 with a baseline is strict enough without splitting every spec for show. |
| Failing the build on today's state | Would block all work until about 50 files are split; the baseline makes the rule apply to new code immediately.      |

**Follow-ups / caveats:** the oversized TypeScript, Rust, CSS and test files are now split (`index.css` imports partials from `src/styles/`). Six crowded folders remain in the baseline (`src/lib`, `src/editor`, `src/state`, `src/state/project`, `src/ui`, `app/shell`) and are reorganised into feature subfolders next. A Rust `clippy::too_many_lines` lint is deferred: `import_docx/body.rs` `paragraph` (138 lines) would fail `-D warnings`. Function length (120), complexity (20), parameters (5) and nesting depth (4) are errors too.

**Reorganisation (2026-11-05):** the six folders that broke the 20-file limit were split into feature subfolders with `git mv` and a codemod that rewrote every relative import, dynamic `/src/...` import, `vi.mock` path, config path and doc reference: `lib/{audio,export,font,import,page,scenes,comments,toc,reading,references}`, `editor/{toolbar,find,reading,pageview,paste}`, `state/{view,pages,app}` and `state/project/output`, `ui/{forms,feedback}`, `app/shell/{header,sidebar,native}`. No file contents changed apart from import paths, and the size baseline is now empty.

## Startup bundle: lazy exporters, importers and import dialogs (2026-11-06)

**Decision:** the workspace libraries that only pure functions use are marked `"sideEffects": false` so the bundler drops what an import does not use, and three heavy paths load on first use: the one-shot exporters (`state/project/output/formats/{book,manuscript,pdf}.ts`, wrapped by `lazyActions` in `exports.ts`), the import pipeline (`lib/import/importBook.ts`, imported inside the two `importBook` actions in `state/project/book/open.ts`) and the import dialogs (`app/dialogs/LazyImportDialogs.tsx` wraps them in `React.lazy`, mounted on first open or first import report). The main script went from 2,067 KB (661 KB gzip) to 1,112 KB (359 KB gzip) plus a 506 KB shared chunk (TipTap, ProseMirror) and a 173 KB chunk (the HTML parser, kept because pasting converts HTML synchronously); the Markdown parser (about 130 KB) and the EPUB, Word, Rich Text and outline writers no longer load at start. Runtime budgets were re-measured after the refactor and are unchanged (typing in a 20k-word chapter: median 29.8 ms; 100k-word Page view: first pagination 48 ms, typing median 29.7 ms; Rust `perf:huge-book` within 3x of baseline).

| Rejected                                                       | Why not                                                                                                                                             |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Loading the HTML paste converter on demand                     | ProseMirror's paste handler must decide synchronously whether to take over; an async load would let the default paste run first on the first paste. |
| `React.lazy` for every dialog and screen                       | Dialogs are small next to the libraries behind them, and a Suspense boundary on each adds states to test for little start-up gain.                  |
| `"sideEffects": false` on `editor-schema`, `mcp`, `project-fs` | `editor-schema` registers TipTap extensions and keeps the asset resolver; the other two are not in the app bundle.                                  |

**Follow-ups / caveats:** the remaining start-up weight is React DOM (about 620 KB of source), TipTap and ProseMirror (about 800 KB), and the app itself. `re2js` (regex Find, about 120 KB minified) now loads the first time the Regex chip is pressed: `loadRegexEngine` in `lib/search/match.ts` resolves before `regex` turns on, so no search runs without the engine (`isInvalidRegex` is false and `compileRegex` returns null until it is loaded; the unit-test setup loads it), taking the main script from 1,112 KB to 989 KB (313 KB gzip). `write_pages_first_save` takes about 6 s wall time for 500 pages in the Rust harness (775 ms CPU); it is fsync time, left as it is because the temp file and rename writes are the data-safety rule.

## Link and note references (2026-11-06, format 12)

**Decision:** a reference is a `file`, a `link` or a `note` (`kind` in the manifest list, format version 12). A link keeps an http or https address in `url`; a note keeps up to 20000 characters in `text`. Neither has a file, so neither goes through the trash: removing one drops the list entry and Book Undo puts it back (a file reference still moves its file to `.cadabra/trash/`). `@cadabracraft/format` owns the rules (`normalizeWebUrl`, `addLinkReference`, `addNoteReference`, `validateReferences`) and the migration (version 11 books get `kind: "file"` on every reference); the desktop app, the headless `cadabra_reference_*` tools and the Rust command share them. A link opens in the default browser only when the author clicks Open, through `open_reference_url`, which checks the address again in Rust (`checked_web_url`: http or https, a host, no spaces or control characters, at most 2048 characters). Nothing is fetched: there is no title lookup, no favicon and no preview, so the "no network calls" rule holds.

**Drag and drop.** Reordering moves onto the shared pointer drag (`ui/useDragReorder`, also used by the chapter list, the Contents list and the scene board), which makes Escape or a cancelled pointer drop nothing; the panel's own copy of that code is gone. The panel shows when something is over it (`data-drop-active`, an outline, a filled drop zone and the words "Release to add files, links or text", never colour alone). Files still arrive through the window's drag-drop events (`onFileDrag`: enter and over set the highlight, leave and drop clear it), because only that event carries absolute paths. Addresses and text arrive as DOM drag events on the panel (`text/uri-list`, `text/plain`) and become links and a note (`parseDropped`); a `file:` or `javascript:` address is never kept as a link. Every drag has a non-drag route (WCAG 2.5.7): the Add menu, the row menu and the grip's arrow keys.

| Rejected                                                      | Why                                                                                                                       |
| ------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Store a link or note as a file in `references/`               | A `.url` or `.txt` file is opened by the operating system, not by us; titles, search and edits would need a second index. |
| Keep the version at 11 and make `file` optional               | A version 11 app would fail or mangle a list item with no `file` on its next save; the bump makes it refuse the book.     |
| Fetch the page title or favicon for a link                    | A network call the author did not ask for; the title is the host until the author types one.                              |
| Open any scheme the author pastes (`mailto:`, `file:`)        | `file:` and custom schemes run local programs; `mailto:` has its own place (a chapter link mark).                         |
| HTML5 drag sources for reordering rows                        | Still rejected (no touch, no keyboard, inconsistent in the webview); the panel only accepts HTML5 drops from other pages. |
| `dragDropEnabled: false` so every drop arrives as a DOM event | The DOM drop of a file has no absolute path, which `import_reference` needs.                                              |

**Not verified.** Whether addresses and text dragged in from another application reach the panel as DOM drag events on every platform while the window's drag-drop handler is on (macOS, Windows and Linux differ): drags from inside the app (selected text or a link in the editor) and the Add menu always work, and a platform that hands such drags to the window only drops files. Check in a release build on each system (see roadmap).

## Typing in one 20,000-word paragraph in Page view: measured (2026-11-06)

The edge-case audit guessed about 400 ms a key because pagination "re-measures every block on an edit". Measured on the current code (Playwright Chromium, Vite dev server, Apple M1 Max, a chapter of a heading and one 20,000-word paragraph, 30 keys 500 ms apart; latency is keydown to the second animation frame; pagination task is the long task after a pause):

| Chapter                    | View                                | Key latency median / worst | Pagination task                |
| -------------------------- | ----------------------------------- | -------------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| One 20,000-word paragraph  | Pages (1400 px wide)                | 117 ms / 126 ms            | about 180 ms once per pause    |
| One 20,000-word paragraph  | Reflow (390 px wide, no pagination) | 117 ms / 132 ms            | none                           |
| 500 paragraphs of 40 words | Pages                               | 33 ms / 52 ms              | below the 50 ms long-task mark |
| 500 paragraphs of 40 words | Reflow                              | 31 ms / 53 ms              | none                           |

An earlier run on an older checkout gave 105 to 115 ms a key and 170 to 190 ms a pagination task, so nothing regressed and the 400 ms figure was wrong. The per-key cost is the same with pagination off: it is the browser laying out and painting the one huge paragraph that the edit changed (ProseMirror's DOM update plus the text layout), not our code. The pagination task is two forced layouts of that paragraph (the `pv-natural` class toggle and reading the page tops), run once per typing pause.

**Decision:** change nothing. **Rejected:** a per-block height cache. The block style cache (`pagination/cache.ts`) already skips every unchanged block, which is why the many-block chapter is cheap, and in the slow case there is only one block, and it is the one that changed, so a height cache would always miss. Making it faster would need splitting a paragraph's measurement (measuring only its changed lines), which would risk page breaks that differ from the browser's and is not worth ~180 ms once per pause on a book-sized paragraph nobody writes.

## Smart typography: quotes, dashes and the ellipsis as the author types (2026-11-06)

**Decision:** an opt-in typing aid (wave 1: logic only; the Settings row and a convert-selection menu item come next). Off by default, app-wide (`smartTypography` in `state/view/editorPrefs.ts`, `cadabracraft.settings`; never in the project, so no format change).

- **The stored text is the final glyphs.** Typing `"` writes `“` or `”`, `'` writes `‘` or `’`, `--` an en dash, `---` an em dash (the third hyphen turns the en dash into an em dash), `...` an ellipsis. Every exporter writes the characters as they are, so `packages/export-pdf/src/escape.ts` is unchanged: it escapes `--`, `---` and `...` as typed ASCII, which no longer occurs once the glyphs are stored. Rejected: converting in the exporters (the editor would show one thing and a file another, Find and the word count would disagree with the print, every exporter and the MCP server would need the rule, and a typed `--` the author meant literally could not be kept).
- **Pure module `packages/editor-schema/src/typography/`**, one responsibility per file: `quoteStyle` (a per-language table: en, de `„ “ ‚ ‘`, fr `« »` with narrow no-break spaces, es `« »` with `“ ”` nested, it `« »`, he and ar the English curly pair, English for anything else, and Swiss de-CH and fr-CH as `« » ‹ ›` without spaces), `decide(prev, quote, style, rest?)` (opens at the start of a block, after whitespace, an opening bracket, a dash or an opening quote of the style; closes otherwise; `’` between letters or after a letter; `’` for a leading elision such as `'tis` and `'90s`; the straight character stays after a digit, so `5'10"` is feet and inches), `convertText(text, style, context?)` (all of it on existing text, idempotent) and `convertSelection(editor)` (a command with no menu item yet: it skips the code mark, code blocks and `lang`-marked passages, and is one undo step).
- **Hand-written TipTap input rules** in an `Extension` (`SmartTypography`) exported from the package but **not** in the shared `extensions` list, so exporters, tests and the MCP server see the same schema as before (it adds no node or mark). The desktop `Editor.tsx` always adds it with two getters, `enabled` (the setting) and `language` (the edition language), read on every key: switching the setting or the language needs no new editor, which would have reset the document to its saved text. TipTap itself runs no rule in a code block, next to the code mark or while an IME composes (`view.composing`); the code mark that is only stored (the first key typed in an empty paragraph with code switched on) is checked by our handler too. The quote style comes from the `lang` mark at the caret, else the edition language. Backspace right after a conversion restores the typed characters (TipTap's `undoInputRule`; all the rules are undoable). The handler replaces with `replaceWith`, not `insertText`: for a quote that is all the content of an empty paragraph, `insertText` replaces the whole paragraph and the caret jumps to the start of the book.
- **Find treats straight and curly quotes as equal** (`fold` in `lib/search/match.ts` maps `‘ ’ “ ”` to `' "`, one code unit each so offsets still hold). Word counting splits on whitespace, so `don’t` and `said—twice` stay one word (pinned by a test).
- **Rejected:** `@tiptap/extension-typography` (a new dependency, fixed English glyphs with no per-language table, no `lang`-mark awareness, em dash for `--`, and rules for symbols such as `(c)` and fractions that a book editor must not change); the Typst `smartquote` (it only changes the PDF, and only for text Typst sees as plain, so the editor, EPUB, DOCX and RTF would still show straight quotes); a ProseMirror plugin with its own undo (more code than reusing `undoInputRule`).
- **Caveats:** a closing single quote after a letter in German, French and the like is typed as `’` (the apostrophe) because the text after it is not known while typing; `convertText` knows it and gives the language's closing mark. `5'10"` and a quote after any digit stay straight, so `"I have 3"` closes with a straight mark. Not verified: IME composition in a native build (only a Chromium CDP composition in Playwright), WKWebView and WebView2.

**Wave 2 (UI):** Settings > Editor has the switch "Smart quotes and dashes" (after Typewriter scrolling). Format > Convert Quotes and Dashes is the toolbar's menu-only item `convertQuotes` (command `editor.convertQuotes`, so the Format menu and the command palette get it from the same list as the other format commands); it is enabled only while the editor shows the toolbar and the selection is not empty (`hasSelection` in `useToolbarState`), calls `convertSelection`, closes the undo group first (`closeHistory`, so one Undo reverts only the conversion even right after typing) and announces "Converted quotes and dashes in the selection" or "No straight quotes or dashes to convert in the selection" in a plain `aria-live` region of the toolbar. That region is deliberately not `role="status"`: the page has one status (Saved) that specs and readers look for. Rejected: a rebindable shortcut (the editor's key commands are a fixed list in `editor-schema/src/keys.ts`; adding one is not a one-line change, so it waits); showing the item in More formatting (that menu is full, see "Toolbar overflow menu").

## Drop a picture on the editor; the Saved notice (2026-11-07)

**Drop.** Tauri delivers a native file drop to the page through `onDragDropEvent`, wrapped by `bridge.onFileDrag`. The bridge now also reports where the pointer is (`DropPoint`, converted from physical to CSS pixels). Two hooks listen: `useReferenceDrop` (References panel) and `useEditorFileDrop` (`editor/drop/`, rendered by `EditorFileDrop` inside the page area). Exactly one acts, decided by `overEditor(at)` (`lib/fileDrop.ts`): `document.elementFromPoint` inside an element marked `data-editor-drop` (the editor root, toolbar included) means the editor; anywhere else belongs to the panel, as before. Hit-testing the element (not a rectangle) means a panel drawn over the editor (tablet drawer) still wins.

- The editor takes the first file with a supported image extension (png, jpg, jpeg, gif, webp, svg: Rust `IMAGE_EXTENSIONS`), imports it with `importImage(path)` and opens the Add image dialog through `startImageFromPath` (shared with the toolbar's `addImage`), so alt text or the decorative flag is still required. Other files: a warning "Only one picture is added at a time. N other files were left out." Files with no image extension (and folders) show "This file type can't be added here."; the warning clears after 8 seconds or on the next drag. A folder named like an image reaches `import_asset`, which refuses it (header alert).
- Rust `record_drop` still records every dropped file as `PickKind::Reference` and now records the first dropped image as `PickKind::Image` too, so `import_asset` keeps requiring a pick: a page cannot import a path the author did not drop or choose.
- A blank (read-only) page ignores drops, Read mode has no editor so nothing there acts, and focus mode works (the dialog lives in `EditorFileDrop`, not in the hidden toolbar).
- Rejected: one window-level listener that routes by file type (the panel also takes images as references, so position is the only honest signal); a plain rectangle test (wrong under overlays); inserting the picture without the dialog (skips alt text).
- Not testable in Playwright: the operating system's drag. The tests send the events Tauri would send.

**Saved notice.** `saveCurrent` shows "Saved" only when no other notice is showing and clears it two seconds after the latest save, only if the notice is still exactly that text (`SAVED_NOTICE_MS`, one timer restarted by each save). A conflict-copy notice (chapter save, `writePage`, `saveManifest`) is set as before, is never replaced by "Saved" and stays until dismissed. Rejected: a general notice timeout (failures must stay).

## Scene filters: one view-only filter shared by the board and the timeline, remembered per book (2026-11-07)

**Decision.** The Scene board and the Timeline share one filter (`SceneFilter`: POV, status and label lists plus a search text) and one "Colour by" choice (None, Label, Status). `lib/scenes/sceneFilter.ts` is pure: `facetsOf` counts distinct values case-insensitively on the trimmed text and prints the first spelling met ("" is a "none" pseudo-value), `matchScene` is OR within a facet and AND across facets, and the search text is a fuzzy `rank` over title and synopsis. `filterBoard` hides rows from the board data and never deletes or reindexes anything; the timeline is filtered after `buildTimeline`, so an undated scene still "follows" the scene before it in the book even when that scene is hidden. `labelColour` maps a lower-cased label by a stable hash onto three existing tokens (accent, warn, success); the label text is always printed on a chip, so colour is never the only signal (WCAG 1.4.1). The filter lives in `state/view/sceneFilter.ts`, remembered per book folder in localStorage (tolerant parsing, 50 books, capped values), never in the project files. While a filter is on, the board hides the grips and the card menu's move items and says so, because `useSceneMoves` indexes are positions in the whole chapter, not in the cards shown; the Unsorted card is hidden too.

**Rejected.** Filtering inside `buildBoard` or `buildTimeline` (would change the timeline's undated rule and make the move indexes wrong). Keeping moves on and translating indexes (a move among hidden scenes has no clear meaning for the author). A filter per view (the author expects the same narrowing on both). Storing the filter in the project file (it is a view preference, and would make a read-only look a file change). A free colour per label (needs new tokens and a picker; the hash keeps colours stable with no data). Using `aria-live` on the count (existing specs select `[aria-live=polite]`; `role="status"` is the same politeness).

## A11yLens as an extra accessibility gate (2026-11-07)

**Decision:** `@eleven-hits/a11ylens` 1.0.0 (MIT, pinned exactly) is a root devDependency. It is a Node CLI with no programmatic API, so we run it as a command and read its JSON. Two gates use it. `scripts/a11ylens/app-gate.test.mjs` scans the source of `apps/desktop/src` and `apps/website` (repo config in `.a11ylensrc.json`: no errors, score at least 95). `e2e/a11y/a11y-lens.spec.ts` runs its rendered mode (axe plus accessibility tree) against the dev server's pre-backend shell. The CLI's own behaviour (exit codes, formats, config, baseline, rule fixtures) is covered by `scripts/a11ylens/*.test.mjs`, run by `pnpm test:scripts`, so a version bump that changes it fails loudly. It is dev-only and local: it makes no network calls and ships nothing in the app. It adds checks the other engines lack (React source rules such as icon-only buttons, a reading-order and keyboard-walk pass), and its score is heuristic, not a WCAG conformance claim. The rendered mode only sees the shell because the Tauri backend is faked inside Playwright pages; app screens stay covered by the axe, IBM and CodeSniffer sweeps.

**Supply chain:** the package is new, so pnpm added it to `minimumReleaseAgeExclude` in `pnpm-workspace.yaml` for this one version. Remove the entry once the version is old enough, and review the package's source and its three dependencies (`@babel/parser`, `axe-core`, `parse5`) before bumping.

| Rejected                           | Why                                                                                                                  |
| ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Rely on axe, IBM and HTMLCS only   | None of them reads component source, so an icon-only button in a `.tsx` file is found only when a screen renders it. |
| Pa11y or Lighthouse CI as the gate | Both need a rendered page for everything; Lighthouse adds a large dependency for a subset of axe's rules.            |
| Treat its score as conformance     | The tool says it is not one; the gate keeps a floor, and the manual screen reader tests stay.                        |

## Scene-break ornaments: a book-wide manifest key, no version bump (2026-11-09)

**Decision:** the author's scene-break mark is an optional top-level `sceneBreak` key in `cadabra.json`, one id from a fixed list (`asterism`, `three-dots`, `fleuron`, `dinkus`, `em-dash`, `none`). The library is `packages/shared/src/sceneBreak.ts`: each id has a `glyph` (the PDF and the EPUB stylesheet) and a `text` (Word, RTF and Markdown). Absent means the template's own glyph, so no book changes until its author chooses one. Every exporter takes the id as an option (`sceneBreak`) and throws `SceneBreakOrnamentError` for an id the library does not list. Word and RTF default to `#` and Markdown to `* * *`, as they always did, because those exporters never used the template glyph.

**Why:** the mark is cosmetic and book-wide, the same kind of setting as `trimSize` and `fonts`, which were added the same way. Absent keeps every existing book's output byte for byte: the PDF source, the EPUB stylesheet (an override is appended only when a mark is chosen) and the EPUB chapter files are unchanged. Storing it in the manifest rather than the edition keeps it with the template, which is also book-wide.

Rejected:

| Option                                                   | Why not                                                                                                                                                                |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Bump `formatVersion` to 13 (migration, old-version test) | An older app would refuse the book; a version bump for a cosmetic choice is heavy. This matches `trimSize` and `fonts`.                                                |
| Store the mark per edition                               | The mark follows the template and the rest of the look, which are book-wide; a translation would only pick a different mark by mistake.                                |
| Per-template default id in the template data             | The template's glyph is already its default. Renaming it to an id would change the template data shape and the Page view, which reads the glyph, for no output change. |
| Export option only, not saved                            | The choice would be lost between exports and could not be used by the MCP and the app without passing it on each call.                                                 |
